It carries the greeting, the cost summary, the billing basis, any excluded spaces, and the stage names — the things a client reads on a phone. The complete document, with the room-by-room scope and the full Terms, is the attached PDF. Two reasons it is not the whole estimate inline: mail clients strip <style> blocks and do not support CSS variables at all, so the real document renders as unstyled text in Gmail; and rebuilding all eight sections a second way would be a second renderer that drifts from the first. The PDF is produced by the same Apps Script conversion that writes the copy filed to Drive, so the attachment and the filed document cannot be different renderings. The stage names come from _cePhases, the same source as the document — the email cannot promise a stage the estimate did not price.
The notice names the mailbox the draft is in (fixed 2026-09-09). Gmail’s /mail/u/0/ is whichever Google account signed in first in that browser, so on anyone with a personal Gmail open the link landed in the wrong account and the draft looked missing. The app reads the signed-in address off the token and names that mailbox on screen; the link itself stays /mail/u/0/ (see The mailbox address never goes in the draft URL, below). This note used to say the address went in the link, which was tried and taken out. If it opens somewhere unexpected, switch account in Gmail — the draft was created in whichever account was picked in the Google window, and that is the one it sends from.
There is one email button, not two. The plain-text path is still there and the app drops to it by itself — sign-in cancelled, or draft failed — saying which. It is no longer offered as a choice beside the real one: the plain email carries no attachment, and two buttons side by side is an invitation to send the wrong one.
⚠ The plain email is recorded like any send by email (since 2026-09-30). When the Gmail draft fails and the app opens a plain email instead, the row reads “Opened as a plain email — send it from your mail app, then confirm”, the document is filed to Drive as every send files it, and the band offers ✓ I’ve sent it. Until then the fallback recorded nothing: the row went on offering Send, and a send made from the mail app could never be confirmed. The app opened a mail program and saw nothing sent, so that is all it records until you press the tap.
Client documents carry the office line AND the concierge’s mobile (new 2026-09-09). The office is (561) 652-5522 and it is one constant, HAVELLIN_OFFICE_PHONE, read by every footer and every signature — it used to be typed out in five separate documents. The mobile comes from the concierge’s own row in Contractors (§12a): Anthony (617) 650-6588, Ashley (978) 857-5374. Anthony Jr has none recorded and his documents correctly show the office alone rather than an empty label.

The office number used to sit in Anthony’s contractor record as his phone, so the firm’s line was being presented as one person’s number while the other two had none at all. A contractor’s phone is now that person’s mobile, and the firm’s line is never in a person’s row. NON_MOBILE_NUMBERS holds the current office line and the retired (561) 370-4700, so a record copied from an old one cannot put a dead number on a client document labelled as a mobile — matched on digits, so any formatting of it is caught.

To give a new concierge a mobile, put it in the phone field on their Contractors row. It reaches the estimate, the invoice, both emails and the plain-text fallback with no other change.

Naming, and the two files in the Estimates folder

The client-facing name says what the document is (new 2026-09-08). The printed PDF and the email attachment are now Havellin Service Estimate - 1234 Ocean Blvd - Sep 8 2026.pdf. It used to be the surname and the job id — Ellsworth-HVL-0007.pdf — which reads as a database key on a document going to a client. docNames(job, kind) names all five client documents, so the printed PDF, the email attachment and the Drive copy cannot drift apart (estimateDocNames, where this began and which this note used to name, now names only the internal worksheet). The Drive names deliberately carry no date: the Apps Script overwrites by filename, so a dated name would leave a new file behind on every re-file instead of replacing the current one.
⚠ TWO DIFFERENT DOCUMENTS WERE WRITING THE SAME FILENAME, AND THE CLIENT FOLDER GOT WHICHEVER FINISHED LAST. The internal worksheet (room-by-room volume and complexity scores, TC/PS hours, per-room dollars, the walkthrough notes) and the client estimate both wrote <HVL-ID>_Estimate.html into the same Estimate subfolder. On approval both fire, 400ms apart, and the Apps Script deletes by name and re-creates — so on a cold start the order they landed was not the order they were sent, and roughly half the time the file sitting in the client’s folder was our cost breakdown. They are now <HVL-ID> - Havellin Service Estimate and <HVL-ID> - Estimate Worksheet (INTERNAL), and the worksheet carries a red Internal worksheet — not a client document line at the top of its first page. Any job estimated before 2026-09-08 should have its Estimate folder checked — if the file there is a table of rooms and hours, the next approval files the client version beside it. (This used to say to press 📁 File to Drive, a button on the retired Client Estimate tab; every job before launch is dummy data in any case.) Both files are PDFs: uploadHtmlToDrive has always converted, whatever the .html in the function name suggests.
⚠ THE ESTIMATE FILES ITSELF ON APPROVAL — there is normally no button to press. Approving with a PIN writes the client estimate into the job’s Drive Estimates folder automatically. Editing an approved estimate takes it back to draft, so re-approving re-files the corrected version over the same file. Once the estimate is filed, the dashboard carries 📁 Filed estimate, which opens the filed copy: in the band’s tray while an estimate step is lit, and in the strip under the timeline after that. That is the answer to “did that work?”, now and tomorrow. If the automatic filing did not land, sending the estimate files it, and 📁 File estimate to Drive appears only on an estimate that has been sent and is still not filed. (Until the Client Estimate tab went on 2026-09-11 this was a 📁 File to Drive button and a Filed to Drive line on that tab’s approval banner. Before 2026-09-09 the button was always on screen, and pressing it repeatedly is what produced the duplicates described below.)
⚠ RE-FILING REPLACES THE DRIVE COPY; it used to add another one on a Shared Drive. uploadHtmlToDrive removed the previous copy with getFilesByName, which on a Shared Drive — where every estate folder lives — can answer empty for files that are plainly sitting there. The removal step then ran zero times and every save created another file, silently. It now asks the Drive API as well, with supportsAllDrives and includeItemsFromAllDrives, and updates the existing file in place so it keeps its id — the link on the job, and any link already sent to counsel, stays live. Any extra copies under the same name are trashed (Drive’s 30-day undo, never deleted), so the first save after this change collapses a folder that has already accumulated duplicates back to one file and the sync badge says how many it cleared. Same root cause as the thumbnail bug in §10a: a Shared Drive needs to be asked explicitly. Requires an Apps Script redeploy. To inspect or clean the folders by hand, run previewFolderDuplicates in the Apps Script editor and read the log, then dedupeFolderConfirm. Since 2026-09-30b both run from the Run menu with no argument (they used to need a folder id the Run menu cannot pass): they sweep every client folder and subfolder under Havellin Jobs, list each document filed more than once under one name, and the confirm keeps the newest copy and trashes the rest (Drive’s 30-day undo, never deleted). Only PDFs are swept: a photograph is never cleared by name, because two different cheque photos can share one. A one-line function passing a folder id still narrows it to that folder.
⚠ THE MAILBOX ADDRESS NEVER GOES IN THE DRAFT URL — got wrong twice. /mail/u/<address>/ was tried percent-encoded (“Your account is not available”) and then with a literal @ (“Temporary Error (404)”). Whatever Gmail does with that segment depends on which Google accounts the browser holds a session for, which is invisible from the app and different on every device — and authorising the OAuth popup grants a token, not a Gmail session. The link is now always /mail/u/0/, the one form that cannot fail, and the mailbox the draft was created in is named on screen instead, with the instruction to switch accounts using Gmail’s own avatar menu. A named mailbox plus a working page beats a URL that guesses and errors. Do not put the address back in the path.
Filing confirms itself, and still says so tomorrow. A filing shows Client Estimate filed to Drive ✓ in the sync badge, and from then on the dashboard's 📁 Filed estimate links straight to the filed copy. Before 2026-08-03 the only acknowledgement was a four-second toast in the bottom-right corner of a long page — missed by anyone watching the button they had just pressed — and nothing anywhere recorded that an estimate had ever been filed. A failed upload records nothing, so the dashboard never claims a save that didn't happen. After an edit, Filed estimate opens the previous version until the manager re-approves; the re-approval replaces it in the same file, so the link stays good. (This note used to describe the retired Client Estimate tab's green ✓ Saved to Drive button and its banner line.)

8. Service Agreement

Client Dashboard → open the job → the Signing packet sent and Agreement signed rows on the timeline (§9a). The Agreement tab's nav button was retired on 2026-09-11. The packet is viewed, printed, sent and filed from the timeline, through the one path every client document takes (§9b). (This used to say the document and every gate were unchanged by the tab's going; the gates have changed since, as the notes below say.)

Which of the two agreement forms you get is decided by the same living/deceased rule as the estimate (§7) — isDecedentJob(job). A deceased client gets the estate form, which is written to an authorised representative signing in a fiduciary capacity; a living client gets the standard form, written to an owner contracting for their own property. You do not choose it, and there is no override.

This changed on 2026-08-03 and it changes what goes out on real jobs. The routing used to name four service types, one of which was the plain Home Cleanout — so a Home Cleanout generated the estate agreement even though it is living-owner work, while Estate Settlement (the deceased engagement) was covered correctly. It now reads the same predicate as the estimate, so the two documents can no longer disagree about who the client is. Any cleanout agreement issued before this date should be re-generated and re-read before it is relied on.
The vendor fee clause follows the fee, as of 2026-09-08. The 15% Service Management Fee came off on 2026-08-02 (§5d) and the estimate, the invoice and the Terms stopped charging it that day — but the standard agreement’s §3.5 went on stating “15% of vendor invoices” and the estate form’s fee table carried a Vendor Management Fee row, in every agreement generated for five weeks. Anthony found it reading a filed copy. Both now read the same constant as the estimate: §3.5 is Vendor Coordination (no fee or markup on vendor invoices; the concierge time is billed under §3.3), the estate fee table names third-party vendors at cost — no fee, and standalone Home Prep keeps its 30% under its own §3.5. A third arm was added 2026-09-10: where the attached estimate carries home-prep vendors on an otherwise labor-based job, §3.5 reads Vendor Coordination and Home Sale Preparation Fee and states the 30% on those vendors — the plain no-fee clause would otherwise have told a Home Editing client that Havellin adds no fee to vendor invoices while Exhibit A charged 30% on the painter, which is the same defect one service over. The estate form’s fee table gains a matching row on the same condition. Any agreement generated between 2026-08-02 and 2026-09-08 promises a fee the invoice will never bill — re-generate before it is signed, and if it is already signed the invoice governs (it charges less, not more).
⚠⚠ A fixed-price agreement states two figures when the job carries prep vendors (2026-09-23). The flat fee is the fixed price, firm; the Home Sale Preparation Fee is charged in addition, at 30% of what those vendors actually invoice. Both forms say so in the fee clauses (standard §3.1, §3.3 and §3.5; the estate form’s fee table and its Fixed Project Fee paragraph). The payment schedule says its amounts include the fee at its estimated figure and are adjusted to the actual amount at the midpoint and final. The termination clause earns the prep fee on the preparation work completed or committed rather than in the flat fee’s stages, and the deposit stays earned on signature and non-refundable. Two older faults came off with it: on a fixed fee both forms said the concierge’s vendor coordination was billed as Transition Concierge time or at the TC rate, which on a flat fee describes a charge that does not exist (it is inside the flat fee), and the “fixed price” they printed was the Havellin total rather than the flat fee. An agreement for a fixed fee saved before the 2026-09-23 change says the prep fee is included in the fixed price, because on that record it is. ⚠ Drafted here and not reviewed by counsel — it rides the review bundle with the rest of the fixed-fee language.
And names the premium and the discount on the fee (2026-09-30, P12). Where the estimate carries them, the fixed-fee clause of both forms adds one sentence each: an expedited-delivery premium of twenty percent (20%) of the fixed price, charged in addition to it, and a preferred-client discount applied to the fixed project fee (less the moving materials it includes, and the premium charged on it). On an hourly engagement the premium sentence now says it is not charged on the Home Sale Preparation Fee. Drafted, not reviewed; in the counsel bundle.

And the fixed-fee premium reaches change orders (2026-09-30, the Q14 follow-up; Anthony: “yes”). The premium sentence now ends “… as itemized on the Estimate, and on the price of any Change Order.” On an expedited fixed-price job a new change order is priced at its hours at the plain rates plus the 20% (§9), and the contract says so before the client signs it. The discount still does not apply to change orders. On a fixed fee saved before 2026-09-30 the premium sits inside the fee: the clause names none, and that job’s change orders keep the plain-rate price. Drafted, not reviewed.
⚠⚠ THREE CLAUSES CHANGED ON ANTHONY’S ANSWERS (2026-09-30, P16). Each is drafted here, not reviewed, and goes to counsel with the rest.
ClauseIt saidIt says now
Standard §3.9, Payment Method (Anthony: “yes” to ACH, wire or check)“Payment may be made by credit/debit card via Contractor’s secure payment link, or by check or ACH payable to Havellin Palm Beach LLC.” — against the no-cards rule: the payment link takes ACH only“Payment may be made by ACH bank transfer through Contractor’s secure payment link, by wire transfer, or by check payable to Havellin Palm Beach LLC.” The estate form names no method, and no other client document mentions a card.
The deposit on an hourly engagement: standard §12.4, estate §8.1 (Anthony: “deposit paid, is lost”)Standard: “… and provide a final invoice. Any deposit will be applied to amounts due. If the deposit exceeds the amount owed, Contractor will refund the unused balance less any non-cancelable costs.” Estate: “The deposit is non-refundable if termination occurs after project start”, a start the form never definedStandard: “… and provide a final invoice for the Services performed and costs incurred through the termination date under Section 12.2. The deposit is applied against that amount. The deposit is earned on signature and is not refundable, including where it exceeds that amount, except where Client terminates under Section 12.3 for a material breach by Contractor that Contractor has failed to cure, in which case Contractor will refund any amount it holds above the amount owed.” Estate, two bullets in place of that one: “The deposit is earned in full on signature of this Agreement and is not refundable. It is applied against the hours worked and materials used, and no part of it is returned where it exceeds them” and “Where Havellin is in material breach of this Agreement and has not cured that breach within seven (7) days of the Client’s written notice describing it, Havellin will refund any amount it holds above the hours worked and materials used through the date of that notice”
The prep fee on a vendor a change order adds: standard §3.5, the estate form’s fee table and its Fixed Project Fee paragraph (Anthony: “yes”, §9)The fee was charged on the home sale preparation vendors “identified in the Estimate” (standard) or “identified in Exhibit A” (estate)“… identified in the Estimate or added by Change Order …” and “… identified in Exhibit A or added by Change Order …”, wherever a change order can add one. An older fixed fee that carries the prep fee inside it keeps its words: no change order can add a vendor there.
The fixed arm of both forms already made the deposit earned on signature (What a fixed-price agreement says about termination, below); the hourly arm now says the same, so the deposit Mark lost keeps on an hourly walkaway has its clause (§9, What the agreements say about the money this dialog keeps). Regenerate any agreement produced before 2026-09-30 — prelaunch, so nothing real was signed on the old wording.
⚠⚠ BOTH FORMS SAY HOW TIME IS BILLED, WHEREVER THEY BILL IT (2026-10-02, P18; Anthony: “yes on agreements”). Hours are logged in half hours and the final invoice bills what is logged (§11), so each clause that bills time adds one line, “Time is recorded and billed in half-hour increments, as worked.”
ClauseWhere the line goes
Standard §3.3, Hourly and Project RatesAfter “All rates are inclusive of on-site project oversight, client liaison, and vendor coordination.”
Standard §3.3, Basis of Fee, on a Home Prep job whose estimate quotes declutter hoursAfter “… the Estimate provides for 6.0 hours ($900) on that basis.”

Estate §3.2, the IMPORTANT paragraph on an hourly engagementAfter “Final billing reflects actual hours worked and materials used.”
Neither fixed-fee arm carries it: a fixed fee bills no time. An agreement already signed keeps the words it was signed on. Drafted, not reviewed by counsel.
The signing packet: the agreement with the estimate attached, as of 2026-09-08. Both forms say the Estimate is attached as Exhibit A and the estate form says the agreement is not valid without it, but the app filed them as two documents in two subfolders and nothing stapled them together — a client sent the agreement alone was signing against an exhibit they did not have. Every view, print, send and filing of the agreement now produces one document: the agreement, then the approved client estimate on a new page under an Exhibit A band. There is no way to send the agreement alone. The packet is filed to the job’s Agreement subfolder as <HVL-ID> - Havellin Services Agreement (a PDF) on the first print, send or file, and since 2026-09-11 it is the only file there (see Generate & Send). (It began as a Print Signing Packet button beside the Agreement tab’s Print / Save PDF, filed as <HVL-ID>_Signing_Packet.html beside the bare agreement.) Also fixed in the same build: both agreement forms now read the approved estimate from the store rather than whatever estimate happened to be open on Build Estimate — before, an agreement opened after building a different client’s estimate printed default rates against a total that had come from somewhere else.
What a fixed-price agreement says about termination, new 2026-09-20 when a flat fee became available on every service. Both forms measured the exit in hours worked (“the Client is responsible for all hours worked…”, “a final invoice reflecting actual hours and materials”) while stating three sections earlier that a fixed fee does not vary with the hours worked. So a fixed-price matter terminated half-way had two answers to what is owed and they contradicted each other — live on every fixed-price Estate Settlement before this date, since that service has always been quotable flat. The living-client form was worse: §12.4 promised to “refund the unused balance”, which on a flat fee stopped early hands most of the deposit back.

The fixed arm now earns the fee in stages, against the payment schedule the client already signed. The deposit is earned in full on signature and is not refundable; 75% is earned once the midpoint milestone is reached; the whole fee on completion of the Exhibit A scope. Non-cancelable vendor commitments ride on top. No part of it is calculated from hours, and the clause says so outright. The time-and-materials arm of both forms was left as it was then. Since 2026-09-30 (P16) its deposit is earned on signature too (Three clauses changed on Anthony’s answers, above).

Why not simply “they owe the full fee”, which is where Anthony started. A clause taking the whole contract price regardless of work performed is tested as liquidated damages and survives only if the figure is a reasonable forecast of actual loss made at signing; the full price on a matter stopped at 10% is not, since the cost was never incurred and the crew is free to take other work. The failure mode is the bad one — a court that calls it a penalty strikes the clause outright and drops us to common-law damages, i.e. below what the earn-out would have collected. On probate it is worse again: the representative pays from estate funds and the fee is reviewed, so an over-reaching exit clause can taint the reasonableness finding on the fee itself. In practice the two barely differ — nobody terminates a concierge engagement on day one for no reason, and a mid-job termination separates them only by the last 25%.

“For cause” is defined and curable, and that is the load-bearing line. It is a material breach by Havellin, described in the client’s written notice, uncured after seven days — then and only then is the balance above the portion earned refunded. If cause meant “the client is unhappy”, every convenience termination would be relabelled and the earn-out would protect nothing. On the living-client form this cross-references §12.3, which already carries notice-and-cure; the estate form’s §8.2 does not, so there it is stated inline rather than pointed at a section that does not say it.

⚠ This is contract language drafted here and not reviewed by counsel, and Florida liquidated damages is fact-specific. It goes to the attorney with the firearms protocol before the first real engagement signs on a flat fee.
⚠ The change-order form printed inside the estate agreement (§4.2) follows the billing basis (2026-09-25). It carried Additional Cost Estimate $ and Revised Total Estimate $ on every engagement — a price field, inside a signed contract, on the time-and-materials change order the app itself refuses to price (§9). On time and materials it now asks for the additional hours and the revised estimated hours, and says the hours are billed as worked at the §3.1 rates and that the change order does not itself create a charge. On a fixed price it asks for the Price of This Change (the additional hours at the §3.1 rates) and the Revised Fixed Project Fee. Drafted, not reviewed by counsel — it rides the review bundle (item A8). Regenerate any estate agreement produced before today before it is signed. Since 2026-09-30 an expedited fixed price adds the premium to that line (the Q14 follow-up; Anthony: “yes”): on a rush estimate saved from that date the price is described as “(the additional hours at the rates in Section 3.1, plus the twenty percent (20%) expedited-delivery premium)”, because that is how the app prices the change order (§9). Drafted, not reviewed. Since 2026-09-30 (P16) the form has a row for an added preparation vendor wherever a change order may add one (Anthony: a labour job whose estimate carries a costed preparation vendor, on an hourly quote or a fixed fee with the prep fee on top; never an older fee with it inside, §9): Added Preparation Vendor, which says the vendor bills the Client directly at cost and the Home Sale Preparation Fee in §3.1 is charged on what it actually invoices (in addition to the fixed project fee, on a fixed price). On time and materials the Billing row there no longer says the change order creates no charge: it says the vendor’s fee is charged on what it invoices and that the change order creates no other charge. The form asks the same rule the change-order dialog asks (coPrepVendorsOn). Drafted, not reviewed (bundle B9).
The estate-form agreement follows the documentation scope on the attached estimate (new 2026-09-04). Three places in it described the inventory as a Havellin deliverable — the Scope of Services paragraph, the §5.2 Florida Probate Compliance list, and the midpoint-payment trigger — and each now reads the estimate's Documentation scope (§5c). At None the scope says the inventory and valuation are not within the engagement and remain counsel's; at Capture only it sells the photographed list without valuation and names valuation and appraisal coordination as counsel's; the §5.2 list replaces "Havellin will prepare a documented asset inventory" and "Havellin will coordinate professional appraisals" accordingly, and the §5.3 appraisal row becomes Admit an appraiser engaged by counsel (since 2026-09-30 it names the party §5.2 names: Who admits the appraiser, below). The midpoint payment used to trigger on completion of the asset-inventory phase; it now names the stage the estimate actually has. An agreement generated before the scope was set on the estimate reads as the full-scope form — re-open the estimate, set the scope, re-approve, and regenerate. Since 2026-09-30 an appraiser on the estimate changes the appraisal half of each arm — see An appraiser on the estimate, two notes down.
⚠⚠ The appraisal clauses follow the TIER, not the scope (2026-09-24). Inventory with values and Inventory + appraisals both price at Full, so an agreement reading the scope alone promised Havellin’s appraisal coordination on both. Now §2’s appraisal coordination for all asset categories, the §5.2 appraisal line and the §5.3 appraisal row are Havellin’s only on Inventory + appraisals. On Inventory with values §2 states in bold that the coordination of professional appraisals is not within the engagement, §5.2 says who arranges them, and §5.3 reads Admit an appraiser engaged by counsel (since 2026-09-30, engaged by the party §5.2 names: Who admits the appraiser, below). The top tier’s wording is unchanged, word for word. The midpoint trigger still follows the scope — the stage it names exists on both tiers. Regenerate a values-tier agreement produced before 2026-09-24. The reasoning is in §4. The pricing question it left open was answered on 2026-09-30 (Q20), and the answer changed this wording again — next note.
⚠⚠ AN APPRAISER ON THE ESTIMATE IS HAVELLIN’S TO COORDINATE, ON ANY TIER (2026-09-30, Q20). Anthony: “yes, drop built-in appraiser scheduling.” The documentation step stopped pricing appraiser scheduling (§5c); an appraiser is priced only by its own vendor line, added under Vendors on Build Estimate (§5d), on any tier. An estimate carrying one is quoting Havellin’s coordination of that appraiser, and on Inventory with values the form would have said in bold that appraisals were not within the engagement, beside an Exhibit A that listed and priced one. So who arranges the appraisals now has three answers, and the agreement and the estimate it attaches read the same rule (appraisalDuty): all of them on Inventory + appraisals (the wording above, unchanged); the ones Exhibit A lists, wherever the estimate carries an appraiser line on any other tier; none otherwise (the counsel wording above). On the middle answer:
  • §2, Scope of Services, says “Havellin will coordinate the professional appraisals listed in Exhibit A (Art Appraiser); any other appraisal remains the responsibility of the Client and the estate attorney.” On Inventory with values it replaces the bold carve-out. On Capture only the carve-out shortens to “Valuation of estate assets is not within this engagement” and the sentence follows it. On None it follows the sentence that leaves the inventory to counsel.
  • §5.2 on a probate matter says “Havellin will coordinate the professional appraisals listed in Exhibit A (Art Appraiser) within the 60-day inventory deadline from Letters of Administration issuance. Any other appraisal is arranged by the estate attorney; Havellin will give the appraiser access to the property and be present for the visit on request.” A trust matter drops the deadline and names the trustee or their counsel; a matter with neither names the Client.
  • §5.3’s appraisal row reads Arrange the appraisals listed in Exhibit A, needing the same written approval as the rows around it, for items in those categories, with any other appraiser as scheduled by the party §5.2 names: the estate attorney, the trustee or their counsel on a trust-only matter, or the Client on a matter with neither (one rule, _agrOtherAppraisalsBy, since 2026-09-30; the row named the estate attorney on a neither matter while §5.2 named the Client).
The parenthesis names the appraiser categories the estimate carries. Regenerate an estate agreement produced before 2026-09-30 on an estimate that carries an appraiser. Drafted, not reviewed by counsel.
⚠ Who admits the appraiser when Havellin arranges none (2026-09-30, P16). The third answer above — no appraiser on the estimate, on any tier but Inventory + appraisals — gives §5.3 the row that admits somebody else’s appraiser. It read Admit an appraiser engaged by counsel / Counsel’s instruction / As scheduled by the estate attorney across its three columns (the trustee’s counsel on a trust-only matter), whatever §5.2 had just said: on a matter with neither probate nor a trust §5.2 says “Professional appraisals are arranged by the Client”, and the row under it named counsel and the estate attorney. The row now reads the one rule §5.2 and the listed-appraisals row read (_agrOtherAppraisalsBy), in all three of its columns:

⚠⚠ AND IT ASSERTED A PROBATE PROCEEDING ON EVERY ESTATE IT EVER PAPERED, UNTIL 2026-09-21. §5.2 read the documentation scope and nothing else, so a matter recorded Trust — which on this book is the common case, not the edge one — signed a contract headed Florida Probate Compliance, promising an inventory “required under Florida Statute §733.604” and appraisals “within the 60-day inventory deadline from Letters of Administration issuance”. A trust administration has no Letters and no 60-day deadline. The clause described a proceeding that does not exist, in writing, on the document the client signs. The heading, the lead and every clause now follow the matter type as well (§4).
What each matter type now signs. Probate — unchanged, word for word. Trust — 5.2 Florida Trust Administration Support, a schedule of the property held by the trust at its carrying value, appraisals arranged by the trustee or their counsel, and the clause that keeps Havellin out of fiduciary accounting work: Havellin does not prepare trust accountings and does not act as trustee, co-trustee or fiduciary, supporting the §736.08135 accounting rather than constituting one. Both — the probate clauses, plus that boundary, because a pour-over will genuinely has both. Neither — it says plainly that no statutory inventory is required. ⚠ The subsection keeps its number on every one of them, because removing a numbered subsection renumbers Termination and everything under it against agreements already issued citing them.
⚠ An UNANSWERED matter type signs the probate wording, byte for byte. That is the safe direction and it is what protects every estate papered before the field existed: stripping a §733.604 promise off a real probate matter on the strength of a question nobody was asked is the bad failure. A test compares the two, clause by clause. ⚠ And the approver noun follows it everywhere — the Scope of Services paragraph, the §5.1 representations, and five cells of the §5.3 authorisation table all read Written PR approval on forms that issue on matters with no PR (§6.3, §7.1 and the signature page still did until 2026-10-03; see the next note). One noun now, so the table cannot say PR three inches under a §5.2 that says successor trustee. A trust matter also gains its own authority warranty in §5.1, which the form asked for nowhere.
The title names the service without “Probate” where the matter has no court (new 2026-10-05, P20; Anthony’s answer to Q26). The line above Client Engagement Agreement and the sentence under it (“This Agreement governs … services provided by Havellin Palm Beach, LLC …”) name the service through the one namer every client document reads (§4). On a matter recorded Trust or Neither a Probate service reads Estate Settlement and a Contested Probate one Contested Estate Settlement; on the probate track (Probate, Both, or not answered) they keep Probate Estate Settlement and Contested Probate Estate Settlement. The price is the service’s own either way. The estate form’s own name map had no Contested Probate entry, so a Contested Probate form on a client stored without a label read Estate Services; the standard form’s map had no Home Cleanout, which fell back to the label stored at intake ([SERVICE TYPE] with none). Both read the catalogue now. The trust’s date is spelled out in §1.2: The Adler Family Trust, dated March 3, 2015 (Q27; it read Mar 3, 2015), as on Exhibit A and the Trust Schedule. Drafted, not reviewed by counsel.

Until today a trust-only matter’s agreement still named Estate of <decedent> as the party, a Letters of Administration row, a Probate Case Number and a Court to fill in, an Estate Attorney, Client / Personal Representative in §6.3 and on the signature page, a co-PR co-signer, and the Personal Representative three times in §7.1. Regenerate a trust or Neither agreement produced before 2026-10-03. Drafted, not reviewed by counsel.
Every co-representative signs (new 2026-10-03, P19; Anthony’s call 4), in DocuSign since 2026-10-05 (P20). Each co-executor or co-trustee recorded on the client gets a Co-Signer block of their own on the signature page with their printed name and recorded role filled in; with none recorded, the one blank block stays. §5.1 gains one representation where any is recorded: “The Client acts together with the co-representative named on the signature page. That co-representative has signed this Agreement, or the Client holds written authority to bind them to it, and every written approval this Agreement requires is given by the Client and the co-representative together.” (with several: Each of them … every co-representative). ⚠ Since 2026-10-05 (P20; Anthony, Q22) each co-signer block carries its own DocuSign signature and date: invisible markers in white text, like the client’s, numbered for the co-signer (/hcs1/ and /hcd1/ for the first). On the DocuSign route each co-representative is on the envelope beside the client and signs there (§8a), and DocuSign reports completed only once every one of them, the client and Anthony have signed; on the sign-by-hand route they sign the same lines on paper and the page is filed from the timeline (§8). The page itself says nothing about how they sign, so it reads true on both routes, and the blank block (nobody recorded) still carries no field. (Until 2026-10-05 the co-signer blocks carried no DocuSign field: each co-representative signed a printed copy, nothing recorded it, and DocuSign’s completed recorded the agreement signed on the Client’s and Anthony’s signatures alone.) Drafted, not reviewed by counsel.
§5.4 No Purchase by Havellin, on every estate form (new 2026-10-03, P19; Anthony’s call 6: the firearms rule, extended to all estate property). “Havellin, its owners, team members and contractors will not purchase or otherwise acquire any of the contents of the property, or any other tangible personal property of the estate or the trust, whether directly or through any other person, and will take no commission, percentage or other share of the proceeds of any sale of that property.” It is the last subsection of §5, so nothing renumbers, and it is limited to the contents and other tangible personal property because §2.1 leaves real property sales to a separate agreement. The standard (living-client) form is unchanged. §7.1 also says now what the representative signs off at the close: “At the close of the engagement Havellin delivers the Disposition Ledger, the Project Records’ final statement of where every item went, to the <approver> for review and signature.” Both are on every estate form, the unanswered one included. Drafted, not reviewed by counsel.
THE CLIENT’S YES IS WHAT TRIGGERS THE AGREEMENT, and since 2026-09-08 the app enforces it. The signing packet can be printed, sent or filed only once the estimate is approved and the client’s yes is recorded with ✓ Client accepted — mark won on the timeline (§9a). One rule decides it: the band asks it before offering a button, and every print, send and file asks it again. Until then the packet can only be viewed, marked PREVIEW (see Generate & Send), and an ACH link is refused as well. There is no PIN to enter; the agreement’s own PIN went on 2026-09-10. And a yes to a lower price is not a yes to this one (2026-09-29): when a manager approves the estimate at a price above the one the client accepted, the job reads Won · Awaiting Re-acceptance and the packet waits again, until ✓ Client accepted the revised price is recorded (§9a). (This note used to describe the retired Agreement tab: its “Awaiting Client Acceptance” banner, its Approve button and the manager PIN.)

Why it needed a gate rather than a rule. Anthony, walking a dummy client: “it looks like an agreement gets pushed into Google Drive before an estimate is even approved … there was an agreement in the client file before we even solidified the estimate.” Approving the agreement was what filed it and the signing packet into the client’s Drive folder (today the first print, send or file stamps the approval and files the packet), and the only thing standing in front of that was our own estimate approval — an internal event that says nothing about whether the client agreed to anything.

Once the estimate is approved you can read the packet, marked PREVIEW until the client says yes, so you can read the contract you are about to talk through. Reading it is free. The first print, send or file is what commits: it stamps the approval and files the packet. An estimate that is not approved yet opens no packet at all. (This used to say the draft rendered the moment you picked a job on the Agreement tab.)
Editing the estimate withdraws the agreement’s approval (new 2026-09-08). Both forms attach the estimate as Exhibit A and the estate form says the agreement is not valid without it — so an agreement approved against a version now being edited is approved against nothing. Until now, editing an approved estimate un-approved the estimate, cleared the job’s approved flag and even cleared the estimate’s Drive stamp, and left the agreement reading Approved for Sending with the PDF button live and a stale Exhibit A already filed in Drive. The Signing packet sent row now says the approval was withdrawn and why, and once the manager re-approves the estimate it says only to send a fresh packet. The next print, send or file after the re-approval stamps the approval again and re-files the packet. Before this build a second approval in the same browser session was suppressed by a once-per-session guard, which is exactly the approval carrying the correction. (This note used to say re-approving re-filed the agreement and the packet by itself, and that the Agreement tab carried the notice. Since 2026-09-29 ✎ Edit estimate is withdrawn once the packet has been sent; after that a change is a change order.)
Edit estimate also takes away the link to the old Drive copy (since 2026-09-30). The estimate’s filing record goes with its approval, so the dashboard no longer offers 📁 Filed estimate over the pre-edit copy as though it were this estimate. The copy stays in Drive; the edited estimate, once approved and sent, is filed over it by name. The same change reaches the other device: a device that had not reloaded does not put the old link back. The badge now reads “Agreement approval withdrawn — once the estimate is approved again, send the client a fresh signing packet”; it used to say to re-approve the agreement, which has no approval step of its own.
⚠ The Approved for Sending band names THIS agreement’s approval (fixed 2026-09-29). The band at the top of an approved agreement printed whichever agreement the open page had approved last. Measured: one client’s agreement approved by Anthony on September 1, then another client’s approved by Ashley today, and the first client’s agreement — the HTML converted for DocuSign — read “Approved by Ashley Jerome” with today’s date; on a freshly opened page an approved agreement showed no band at all. It reads the client’s own record now, on both forms and in the signing packet. The band is internal: it is hidden when printed, and every client document now hides it in the PDF that is sent for signature or filed — before today nothing kept it out of that PDF except a print-only rule the converter may not apply. Regenerate an agreement produced before today rather than rely on the approver its band names.
⚠ THE AGREEMENT’S “EMAIL TO CLIENT” BUTTON HAD NEVER WORKED, so agreements@ had never been copied on anything (fixed 2026-09-08). Anthony: “do we cc estimates and agreements @havellin like before? that’s important.” The estimate always has and still does; the agreement never did. Its button existed in the markup carrying display:none and nothing anywhere set its address or showed it — the same dead-control shape as the deposit button before 2026-07-30. Both this manual and the playbook documented it, which is why it read as working.

It is real now and behaves like the estimate’s: a formatted HTML email as a draft in your own Gmail, CC’d to agreements@havellinpalmbeach.com, falling back to a plain-text email (also CC’d) when the Google sign-in is cancelled or the draft fails. The body carries the payment schedule off the approved estimate; a job with no approved estimate prints no schedule rather than one full of zeroes. Since DocuSign became the firm’s route for agreements (§8a), this email is the 📄 Send as PDF to sign by hand route; on the DocuSign route agreements@ is copied on the envelope (routing order 3) and receives the executed copy. Since 2026-10-05 (P21; Anthony’s answer to Q31), where a co-representative is recorded, its “When you are ready, sign and return it and we will confirm the schedule.” goes on: “Each co-representative named on the signature page signs it too.” The formatted email, its plain text and the plain-email fallback all say it, so a packet does not come back with the client’s signature alone. A client with nobody beside them gets the email as before.

It attaches the SIGNING PACKET, not the agreement alone — the agreement by itself refers to an Exhibit A the client does not have. Sending goes through the same gate as printing (every print, send and file asks agreementReady first), so it cannot be a way past the approval gates. (It used to be a tab button, tested to appear exactly where Print / Save PDF did.)
⚠⚠ THE STANDALONE HOME PREP FORM GAINS AN HOURS ARM WHEN THE ESTIMATE QUOTES DECLUTTER HOURS (2026-09-14). Its §3.3 Basis of Fee has said in bold, since 2026-09-11, that “no Transition Concierge or Property Specialist hours are billed on this engagement”. That is still what a pure vendor-management prep job signs. On a job that quotes declutter hours it would be false on a signed contract whose Exhibit A prices those very hours on its face — the same class of defect as the $150/hour rate card that shipped on the fee-only form until 2026-09-11, running the other way. §3.3 now states both bases: the Home Sale Preparation Fee, and Transition Concierge services at the estimate’s own rate, naming the hours and the amount. “No Property Specialist hours are billed” survives, because it stays true — prep books no specialist hours at any time. §1.2 names the decluttering as a second service and points at §3.3; §3.8 carries the 15% notice threshold against the hours, which reaches a prep engagement for the first time.

A blank template — a prep job with no estimate built yet — still prints the fee-only clause, which is correct: it prices nothing, so it must not print an hourly rate card. (From 2026-09-25 that clause names the concierge rate for hours a change order adds after signing — one rate, attached to that route, not a rate card; see the next note.) Re-generate any prep agreement after changing the declutter hours, or the contract and Exhibit A state different bases.
⚠⚠ AND THE VENDORS-ONLY FORM NAMES THE ONE ROUTE TO HOURS AFTER SIGNING (2026-09-25). Its §3.3 still says in bold that no Transition Concierge or Property Specialist hours are billed on the engagement — true of what the client signs. It now goes on: hands-on work the client asks for after signing, such as decluttering, is billed only if the client signs a Change Order under §3.8 stating the concierge hours, and those hours are then billed as worked at the concierge rate the clause itself states — $150 an hour, $185 on a Premium Estate job — on top of the Home Sale Preparation Fee. §3.8 gained the sentence that makes the cross-reference true — “Hands-on work Contractor is asked to do after signing is documented in a written Change Order signed by both Parties” — because until now it spoke only of vendor work being re-quoted, and a clause pointing at a Change Order that the section it names never mentions is worse than none.

⚠⚠ AND IT STATES THE RATE, SO THE CLIENT SEES IT BEFORE SIGNING (2026-09-25, same day). Anthony: “I think we should mention the hourly rates in the home prep agreement.” The first cut left the rate to the change order, on the reasoning that a contract billing no hours should carry no rate card — which made the page a client signs mid-job the first place they saw one. It is still not a rate card: the rate appears once, attached to the change-order route, never as the basis of the fee. The concierge rate only — the clause says in bold that no specialist hours are billed and a prep change order refuses them, so a specialist rate would price work the contract rules out. The agreement and the change order read one rate definition, so they cannot state two rates for one job; the printed change order now restates the contract’s rate rather than setting it. ⚠ A prep agreement generated before 2026-09-25 has no carve-out — it says only that no hours are billed. The change order the client signs states the hours and the rate and is signed by both parties; whether that is enough on a contract that says flatly no hours are billed is counsel’s question, and it is on the review bundle (B5). Prelaunch, so no client holds one. Drafted, not reviewed.

Since 2026-09-30 a prep change order can also add a vendor (§9), and the agreement’s wording did not change for it. §3.5 already charges the Home Sale Preparation Fee on the vendor costs managed under the agreement, and §3.8 already says work added after signing is quoted and agreed with the client in writing before it proceeds. The change order names the vendor, its estimated cost and the fee at that cost, and once the client accepts it the vendor is billed like the rest.

Generate & Send

⚠ ONLY THE COMBINED DOCUMENT IS RETAINED, as of 2026-09-11. Anthony: “let’s just have the combined doc saved and sent.” Until now the Agreement subfolder received two files — the signing packet and the bare agreement, as <HVL-ID>_Agreement.html. The packet is the document the client signs; the agreement alone is the same terms with its Exhibit A missing, and both forms say the agreement is not valid without it. So the folder held a document that must never go out sitting one click from the one that must, under a nearly identical name.

Nothing is lost by not filing it twice — the packet carries the agreement verbatim as its first page. Measured on a real approval: two files (114,044 and 128,276 bytes) became one, the 128,276-byte packet, unchanged.

⚠ A job papered before 2026-09-11 still has both in its Drive folder. The app replaces its own copy of the packet whenever it files the packet again (this used to say “on the next approval”; there is no approval step now), but cannot remove the other one, and nothing in the app deletes from Drive on its own. If you are tidying a folder by hand, the one to keep is the one whose name reads Havellin Services Agreement; the _Agreement.html one is the half-document.
⚠ THE AGREEMENT’S SECOND MANAGER PIN WAS REMOVED ON 2026-09-10. Do not restore it as a precaution. Anthony: “there are a lot of ‘gates’. maybe too many. once an estimate is approved by a manager and accepted by a client, a TC should be able to send an agreement without further manager approval … there is literally no way to amend an agreement that comes out of the system.”

Why it is safe, written down so nobody adds it back. The agreement’s commercial terms are the approved estimate — attached as Exhibit A, and both forms say the agreement is not valid without it — and the rest is generated from the service type and the job record. There is no free-text field anywhere in it, so there was no version a second manager could read that differed from the one the pricing PIN had already approved. The two facts that genuinely need a human are both already captured against a named person: a manager approved the price, and the client accepted it.

The GATE is unchanged — estimate approved and client accepted, exactly as before. What changed is that meeting it now stamps the approval on the way through the first print, send or file, instead of demanding a separate act. The timeline lost a whole row for it.
  1. Open the job on the Client Dashboard. The packet is built from the approved estimate.
  2. Press ✉ Send for signature — DocuSign on the Signing packet sent row. One press stamps the approval, files the signing packet to the Drive Agreement folder and sends the envelope. DocuSign emails the client, and there is nothing to confirm (§8a). For a client who will sign on paper, by post or in person, press 📄 Send as PDF to sign by hand beside it instead. It stamps and files the same way, and builds a Gmail draft with the packet attached, CC’d to agreements@.
  3. On the paper route only: send the mail, then press ✓ I’ve sent it.
  4. For a packet you hand to the client yourself (since 2026-10-01): print it from the tray, give it to them, then press 🤝 Handed over in person on the same row. It asks first, then records the send on the packet’s own record — today, the name the app attributes sends to, and in person — through the same gate as the send (the estimate approved and the client’s acceptance recorded), stamping the approval and filing the packet if nothing had yet. The row goes green reading Handed over in person, and the band moves to Get the agreement signed with ✓ Record the signed agreement. Nothing is emailed and there is no I’ve sent it.
🖨 Print packet, in the tray, prints a copy to hand over — and does not send it. Printing stamps the approval and files the packet, but it does not record the packet as sent, and a signature can only be recorded on a packet recorded as sent. For a client signing in front of you, print it and press 🤝 Handed over in person. (Until 2026-10-01 the only way to record a packet handed over was the paper route’s Gmail draft. This list used to name “✉ Send signing packet” and a Gmail draft as the route, with printing as the in-person alternative. DocuSign has been the firm’s route since 2026-09-18, and printing has never recorded the packet as sent.)
⚠ FOUR CHANGES TO THE AGREEMENT AND ITS PACKET (2026-09-29).

You can read the packet before the client says yes. Once the estimate is approved, 👁 View packet, in the strip of document links under the timeline (the Signing packet sent row is not lit yet, so its link sits there), opens the signing packet titled Signing Packet — PREVIEW, so you can walk a client through the contract they are deciding on. Print and Send still wait for Client accepted — mark won (or, after a raise, Client accepted the revised price): the viewer carries no Print button, and viewing stamps nothing and files nothing. An estimate that is not approved yet opens no packet at all.

The fee clause names the rush premium and the discount. Both forms add one sentence each where the estimate carries one. On an hourly engagement they go in §3.3 on the standard form and under the fee table (§3.1) on the estate form:
“At Client’s request this engagement is scheduled on a priority basis, for which an expedited-delivery premium of twenty percent (20%) of Contractor’s fees[, other than the Home Sale Preparation Fee,] is charged, as itemized on the Estimate and applied to the fees actually billed.”
“A preferred-client discount of ten percent (10%) applies to Contractor’s labor fees [and to the expedited-delivery premium charged on them], as itemized on the Estimate and applied to the labor fees actually billed.”
The estate form says Havellin where the standard form says Contractor. The first bracket appears where the engagement carries that fee (an estimate saved from 2026-09-30), the second only on a rush job. On a fixed fee saved from 2026-09-30 the fixed-fee clause names both instead (see And names the premium and the discount on the fee, above); on an older fixed fee both sit inside the fee the clause already states. (This note used to say the sentences appeared only on an hourly engagement.) Drafted, not reviewed — on the counsel list as B6.

No materials package reads as none. With nothing quoted, both forms say “No moving or packing materials package is quoted on the Estimate, and none is billed.” They used to print “(None — $0)” inside a sentence describing a package.

A discount changes the price, so it takes the packet’s approval with it. Offering, changing or removing a discount now revokes the agreement’s approval along with the estimate’s, the Signing packet sent row says why, and the next print or send re-files the packet at the new price — before today Drive kept the packet with the old Exhibit A. Once the manager re-approves, that row says only send a fresh packet (2026-09-30; it went on saying to re-approve the estimate until the packet was sent). Once the packet has been sent, Offer discount and ✎ Edit estimate are both withdrawn: the client is reading and being asked to sign that price, so a change after that is a change order (§9). Edit estimate was the second door (2026-09-29): it stayed until the signature, and pressing it un-approved the estimate and the packet together. Both buttons ask one rule, which reads a DocuSign send as well as an emailed one; pressing either anyway is refused with the reason, and Build Estimate reads Estimate Locked — Signing Packet Sent.
⚠ A GMAIL DRAFT MADE BEFORE A DISCOUNT IS FLAGGED, NEVER SENT (2026-09-29). Anthony: “just flag a previous Gmail draft if a discount is offered.” A packet sent by email is a draft in your own Gmail until you send it and tap ✓ I’ve sent it, and nothing reaches back into a draft once it is made — so a discount offered in between leaves a draft carrying the old price. On the build before this the row then offered only I’ve sent it and Open the packet draft, both over that draft. Measured through the real buttons: a packet drafted at $11,750, a 10% discount, the manager’s PIN — and one tap recorded the $11,750 packet as sent.

What happens now:
• Before you apply it, the discount pop-up names the draft — the day it was made and the Gmail account it is in — and says to delete it and send a fresh packet once the manager re-approves.
• After, the confirmation says the same in amber, on the dashboard.
• The Signing packet sent row stops offering I’ve sent it and the link to the old draft, names the draft to delete, and offers Send again once the price is re-approved.
• The fresh send says which of the two drafts to delete, and the line goes once the fresh packet is confirmed sent.
The app never deletes anything in Gmail — the draft may be in a colleague’s mailbox, and deleting mail is for a person to do knowingly. Editing the estimate does the same to a draft made before the edit, and both changes flag an estimate or invoice draft exactly as they flag the packet. It works across two devices: the flag rides on the job, so a discount offered on the other laptop still flags a draft made on this one.

⚠ Already sent it from Gmail and forgot the tap? Do not apply the discount. Cancel, tap I’ve sent it, and the price is then a change order — the pop-up says so. A discount offered before today set no flag, so a draft made before an older discount is not named anywhere: if you discounted a job whose packet was drafted and never sent, look in Gmail for it.
⚠ THIS NOTE USED TO DESCRIBE A DIFFERENT BUTTON. Until 2026-09-18 Generate Payment Link asked a separate Apps Script for a link and mailed it to billing@, and nothing ever read back from Stripe. It is 🏦 ACH payment link now, it sits on the deposit row of the timeline, and the payment records itself when the money lands. See §8b.

Then, as each thing actually happens

Each step appears only at its own row on the timeline, records who did it and when, and refuses to run out of order — you cannot record a signature on an agreement that was never sent.

  1. ✓ I’ve sent it — you have actually sent the packet (§9b). ⚠ On a DocuSign job this step does not appear: DocuSign does the sending, so there is nothing for you to confirm (§8a).
  2. The signature comes back. Which button you see depends on how the packet went out, and the app decides, not you:
    • Sent through DocuSign — the row reads DocuSign is watching for it and the signature arrives on its own. ↻ Check DocuSign now is there for the moment you have watched the envelope complete and want the app to look straight away (§8a). On an estate each co-representative signs in the same envelope, beside the client, and the row names them once it completes (since 2026-10-05).
    • Sent as a PDF, or handed over in person — ✓ Record the signed agreement, below. On an estate with co-representatives, then file the page they signed (File the page signed by …, under the timeline; the note below).
    Signing does not imply payment; these are separate facts and the app keeps them separate.
  3. ✓ Record payment — see below. This is what allows work to begin.

The signature record

⚠ UNTIL 2026-09-11 THE APP HELD NO RECORD OF WHO SIGNED AN AGREEMENT, AND THE NAME IT SHOWED WAS OURS. Mark Agreement Signed stamped the Havellin manager who had approved the price, so the dashboard read “Agreement signed · Anthony Graziano” over a contract Anthony did not sign. This has nothing to do with e-signatures — it was live on every wet signature. On a court-reviewed probate matter it is exactly the question counsel asks: who bound the estate to this? “Our managing partner” is not an answer.

✓ Record the signed agreement now asks four things:

FieldNotes
Who signed it?The client. Prefilled with the estate’s authorised representative, or the client on a living-owner job. A blank is refused — a signature record with no signer on it is the state this replaced.
How did it come back?Signed in person · Signed copy returned.
Date signedThe date they signed, not the date you typed it in.
NoteFree text — e.g. where the original is held.
The signer and the recorder are two fields. Who signed is the client, typed in here or reported by DocuSign. Recorded by is the manager who approved this job’s price, because the app has no sign-in and cannot know who pressed the button; on an envelope it is DocuSign. The old field held the same approver. Both are kept, with both dates. (This note used to say the recorder was whoever typed the signature in; the app has never known that.)
An e-signature carries the client’s own day and address (since 2026-09-30). DocuSign reports when the client signed and the email address it authenticated them at. The record keeps both, and the Agreement signed row reads Electronic signature · <their address> beside that day. Until then the date recorded was the day the whole envelope completed — Havellin’s countersignature, often days after the client’s — and the address was dropped. The day is the calendar day here: a client signing at 9:30pm is recorded on that day, not the next. A signature recorded by hand names no address.
The co-representatives’ signatures (since 2026-10-05, P20; Anthony’s answer to Q22). On an estate with co-representatives recorded, the agreement is signed by each of them as well as the client, and the record holds each one. Through DocuSign each co-representative signs beside the client, and when the envelope completes the signature record keeps each one’s name, address and own day (the calendar day here, as the client’s is); the Agreement signed row names them with the client (Ruth Adler, Daniel Adler) and adds Daniel Adler signed in DocuSign with his day. On the sign-by-hand route (a PDF to sign by hand, or the packet handed over in person) you record the client’s signature as before; the notice then names the co-representative whose signature is not on record yet, and the row flags it in amber, on the desk’s track and the phone’s rail: Not on record: the signature of Daniel Adler. File the page they signed, under the timeline. Under the timeline, above the payments, Co-representatives on the agreement names each one and offers File the page signed by Daniel Adler: pick the PDF, or a photograph of the signature page they signed, and it is filed to the client’s Signed Records folder as a Co-signed agreement page (a signed record, kept like a signed release approval) naming them. The flag clears, the row names them, and the filed page links to Drive with a Void for one filed against the wrong paper (voided, the flag comes back). A flag, never a refusal: nothing waits on it, and the job activates on the client’s signature and the deposit as before. One rule answers whether every co-representative’s signature is on record, and the row, the block and both notices read it. (Until 2026-10-05 nothing recorded a co-representative’s signature at all.)
The signature page names who signs before the work begins (since 2026-10-05, P21; Anthony’s answer to Q30). It read “No work will begin until both signatures are obtained and the deposit has been received.” With co-representatives signing on the same page, “both” could be any two of three. It now reads “No work will begin until the Client and Havellin have signed and the deposit has been received.” That is what the app waits for: the job activates on the client’s signature and the deposit, and a co-representative not yet on record is flagged on the Agreement signed row, never waited on (§5.1’s joinder covers them). The acknowledgment beside it (both parties) is unchanged: the parties are still two. Drafted, not reviewed by counsel.
A job signed before 2026-09-11 is still signed, and its signer is unknown. That is a fact about the world rather than about our records, so it reads as signed with nobody named and the row says “Signed before the signature record — who signed it is not on file”. It does not print the old name as the signer, because that name is ours.
⚠ Electronic signature is never offered to a person, deliberately — and that is still true now DocuSign is live. The picker lists only the two manual methods and the app refuses anything else, so nobody can type in an electronic signature that no provider issued. A real one is never typed: it arrives from DocuSign, which knows who authenticated and when, and that is the entire point of using a provider.

⚠ THIS NOTE USED TO SAY “no provider is connected today”. That has been false since 2026-09-17 — see §8a. What it predicted is what happened: on a DocuSign job the manual button stands itself down on its own and the row says the provider is watching for it.

Recording a payment

The same button records all three payments — deposit, midpoint and final. A Which payment is this? picker at the top of the dialog opens on the first stage that hasn't been satisfied, so in normal use you never touch it. Pressing ✓ Record payment on a timeline row opens it on that row’s stage, and the dialog’s title and its button name the stage: Record Deposit →, Record Midpoint Payment →, Record Final Payment → (on Home Prep, Record Second Payment →).

Only the deposit gates anything. Recording a midpoint or final payment captures the money so the job's paid total is complete — it doesn't unlock work, and a midpoint cheque of any size will never fund a job. Until the payment recorder was built, midpoint and final money was invoiced and then recorded nowhere at all.
⚠⚠ THE AMOUNT OPENS ON WHAT THE INVOICE ASKS FOR (2026-09-29). Every stage is prefilled with what is still outstanding on it: the deposit with 50% of the approved total less anything already received, and the midpoint and the final with the figure that stage’s own invoice asks for, less anything already recorded against it — the same number as the invoice’s Payment Due Now line, read from the invoice itself rather than worked out a second way. The line under the title says where the figure came from. Check it against the cheque in your hand and change it if the cheque is different; only the deposit challenges a short amount. A final that cannot be priced yet — an hourly job with no hours logged — prefills nothing and says so: type the figure from the invoice you sent.

⚠ Until 2026-09-29 the midpoint and the final opened BLANK, under a button reading Record Deposit → on every stage (workflow audit M6). Pressing it with the amount empty was refused, and the figure you then had to type was on a document on another screen.
⚠ EVERY DATE THE APP FILLS IN FOR YOU IS TODAY WHERE YOU ARE. The date a payment was received, the date the client accepted, the date the agreement was signed, the date on the hours form, the day a job is activated and the day it is closed. Until 2026-09-29, after 8pm Eastern (7pm in winter) every one of them read TOMORROW (workflow audit M7): the app took the date from the UTC clock, which turns over at that hour. So an evening hours entry landed on the next day in the log the final invoice bills from. Anything recorded in an evening before that date is worth a glance; correct a wrong date where it was entered.

Recording the deposit

The deposit is 50% of the approved total and is never waived or varied. Recording it captures evidence, not a tick:

  • Amount — prefilled with what's still outstanding, so a second cheque needs no arithmetic in the field.
  • Date received, method, and reference (cheque number, wire confirmation, Stripe id). The methods are Personal cheque, Cashier’s cheque, Wire transfer, Bank transfer (ACH) and Cash. There is no Card since 2026-09-30 — Havellin takes no card payments, and the recorder refuses one however it is reached. A payment recorded as a card before then still reads Card and keeps the cleared state it was recorded with.
  • Paid by — the trust, estate account, law firm, or the client. In estate work the payer is frequently not the client, and this is what tells you the estate is funding the engagement properly. It also settles the question if heirs later dispute who paid for what.
  • Photo of the cheque — filed to Drive and linked to the payment. Five seconds, and you want it before the cheque leaves your hands. This is what makes a hand-recorded payment a document rather than an assertion.

Partial payments are normal and handled. Two cheques, or a trust sending part and the family the rest, all work: the job stays part-paid and unfunded until the running total reaches the target, the Deposit received row reads Part paid — $X of $Y, and ✓ Record payment stays on it for the next one. A short total asks before it's accepted; a second cheque that completes the deposit passes without a warning.

Work starts on received, not cleared. Waiting for cheques to clear costs three to five days on every job. Wire transfers and cash are recorded as cleared on receipt. A cheque, or a bank transfer you record by hand, is recorded as uncleared: the confirmation says so, and the recorder's list of payments at that stage marks it. It stays uncleared until the bank confirms it and somebody presses ✓ Mark cleared beside it on the dashboard’s payments list (below; this note said until 2026-09-30 that nothing in the app cleared it). A bank transfer Stripe reports is recorded as cleared, because the app records it only once it has settled, and one you recorded by hand is cleared when Stripe confirms it (§8b). At or above $10,000 a personal cheque is no longer the accepted instrument: wire preferred, cashier's cheque accepted. Recording a large personal cheque anyway is allowed, since the money is already in hand. The recorder warns you, and the payment is stored as a policy exception. Like every hand-recorded payment it is attributed to the manager who approved this job's price, because the app has no sign-in to say who pressed the button. (This note used to say uncleared payments showed on the dashboard until the money settled, and that the exception carried your name.)

A certified cheque and a cashier's cheque are not the same thing: certified is drawn on the client's account with the bank only secondarily liable; a cashier's cheque is drawn on the bank's own account with the money already taken. Ask for the second one. Neither is bounce-proof — counterfeit cashier's cheques are a common fraud — so on a large deposit, phone the issuing bank on a number you look up yourself, not the one printed on the cheque.

The payments list: Mark cleared and Void

New 2026-09-30. Every payment recorded on a client is listed under the timeline on the Client Dashboard, headed Payments recorded: the amount, the stage, the method, the day it was received, who paid it, and its state — Uncleared; Cleared with the day and who (or Stripe reported it settled); or Void with the day, who and why. A deposit recorded before payment records existed has no method and reads amount inferred — predates payment records: its amount is the 50% target, not a figure anybody counted, as the recorder also says (its Already recorded at this stage list reads it the same way since 2026-10-06, P22, where it printed · — for the method). The list is there on every state of the job, closed and retained included. Until then no screen listed the payments, nothing cleared a cheque, and nothing took back a payment recorded in error.

  • ✓ Mark cleared — on a payment that counts and is uncleared. Press it once the bank shows the money as final. It asks first, then records today and the manager who approved this job’s price, as every payment is attributed (the app has no sign-in). It moves no money: the job was funded on the money received.
  • Void — on any payment that still counts: a cheque that came back unpaid, a payment typed twice, one recorded on the wrong client. The dialog names the payment and says what the void does to the money before anything is written: what comes off the stage, whether the job stops being funded (and so whether hours can still be logged), whether the timeline will show Deposit voided: record the replacement payment (below), whether the final reads unpaid again, the amount a closed job records as retained, and the job’s received total. A reason is required. The payment is not deleted: it stays on the list, struck through, marked void with its reason, and counts toward nothing — not the deposit, not the invoices’ received rows or balance, not the recorder’s prefill, not the retained amount. Then record the money the way it really arrived.
A void is for good, and a recorded payment is never edited — decided with Anthony, 2026-10-01. There is no un-void and no way to change a payment’s amount, date or method once it is recorded: to correct one, void it with the reason and record it again. Removal is a recorded act, so the wrong entry stays on the list, struck through, beside the right one. On a current deployment (Apps Script 2026-09-30b, §2) a void and a clear also hold against a device that had not reloaded and then changed the same payment.
A deposit voided on a job that had been paid for: a red step, and nothing rewinds (since 2026-10-01). When the void takes the deposit below its target — the cheque that funded the job came back, on a job that may already be Active — the timeline’s Deposit received step is blocked: the band turns red and reads Deposit voided: record the replacement payment, names the payment, the day it was voided and why, and prints the fix under it (press ✓ Record payment when the replacement arrives). The job stays active, Job active keeps its day and its name, and nothing else is undone; hours cannot be logged until a payment that counts restores the deposit, and then the step goes green and the band moves on. It is read from the payments alone, never the job’s status: a void that leaves the deposit whole (a payment typed twice) raises nothing, and a deposit that was never whole reads Part paid as before. (Until 2026-10-01 the band simply went back to Collect the deposit.)

Refunds are on the list too (since 2026-10-01). A refund recorded on an hourly walkaway (Closing out a job that dies, §9) is listed as money that went back — Refund, its method, reference and payee, and Refund sent with the day and who recorded it — and the heading reads Payments and refunds recorded. It counts toward nothing a payment counts toward. It has no Mark cleared; one recorded in error is voided with a reason, exactly as a payment is, and the dialog says first what the retained amount and the refund due become.

Payment Schedule

MilestoneAmountTiming
Deposit50% of estimateDue upon signing (estimate basis — no actuals yet)
MidpointBrings cumulative to 75%, with the home-prep fee trued to actual Job Plan quotesDue at project midpoint (Home Prep: the second payment, once the vendor schedule is booked)
FinalBalance — hourly: labor trued to logged hours, a Change Order's hours included; fixed price: the rest of the fee plus each accepted Change Order's price; fees on actualsWithin 7 days of final invoice

Third-party and home-prep vendor invoices are billed directly to the client at cost. Havellin's 30% Home Sale Preparation Fee on home-prep vendors — on a standalone engagement, on prep bundled into a labor job (since 2026-09-10) and on a fixed-price job, where it is its own line on top of the flat fee (since 2026-09-23) — is calculated on the actual quotes logged in the Job Plan sourcing, including a prep vendor an accepted change order added (since 2026-09-30, §9), and trued up from the midpoint invoice onward; no fee is charged on any other third-party vendor; lines with no logged quote fall back to the estimate, tagged "est." Each stage is generated from its own row on the job timeline (§9a).

⚠⚠ BOTH FORMS NOW COVER THE WRITTEN RECORD, AND UNTIL 2026-09-21 NEITHER DID. Every custody, retention and non-disclosure promise in the two agreements attached to a defined term, Documentation Media, which means photographs and video. Once the item record went to all six labour services (§10a), both engagements began producing a written document naming who took what — a daughter, a charity, an auction house — held for seven years, and none of those promises reached it. The standard form’s general confidentiality clause (§9) is looser again: commercially reasonable efforts, with disclosure permitted to any vendor who needs it, which is not a standard to hold a list of third-party names to. Both forms now define Project Records and put them on the same terms as the images: same folder, same named-access rule, same seven years, never sold or licensed, and expressly outside the marketing clause — a client who does not tick the opt-out is not thereby authorising publication of a list naming their daughter.
Standard form (living client)Estate / probate form
Where it sitsNew §10.1b. §10 is renamed Documentation and Records Authorization — it is no longer only about photographyAppended to §7.1, renamed Documentation Media and Project Records. No new number, so Termination, Dispute Resolution and General Provisions do not shift against agreements already issued citing them
Who may see itThe Client, and anyone they authorise in writingThe PR, counsel of record, anyone the PR authorises — and produced as required by law or court order
Naming a recipientStated as the Client’s own instruction, used for nothing else, and the Client may ask in writing that a name be left outNo such opt-out — a PR cannot suppress a beneficiary’s name from a fiduciary record. Cites the signed receipt §5.3 already requires
What it disclaimsNot an appraisal, not evidence of title, not a tax / insurance / estate-planning recordThe same, plus: not the Fla. Stat. §733.604 inventory, and no evidence that a distribution satisfied a devise or bequest
Three asymmetries are deliberate; do not “harmonise” the two forms. The estate form has a production duty because somebody else is entitled to the record; the living form does not, because a client’s record is theirs. Only the living form lets a recipient go unnamed. And only the estate form carves the record out of §7’s return-or-destroy bullet — “Destroy or return all Client documents upon project completion or written request” sits five bullets above a seven-year fiduciary retention. It reads as covering the estate’s own documents we encounter (the will, deeds, financial records) rather than records we create, but a contract is construed against its drafter and that is not a thing to leave to inference on a matter a court may read.
⚠ NOT REVIEWED, AND THREE THINGS GO TO COUNSEL WITH IT. This language was drafted here, like the rest of both forms, and Anthony is having the whole of both reviewed before the first real engagement signs. Flag these three specifically. (1) The standard form now states two confidentiality standards — §9’s any vendor who needs it against §10.1a/§10.1b’s a vendor performing Services on this engagement; the narrower should govern and §9 may want tightening rather than relying on the specific overriding the general. (2) Seven years was chosen to match the images and is not derived from anything; a written record naming non-parties may warrant a different period. (3) Those non-parties never signed anything, and their names sit in our hands for seven years — worth sizing against the insurance position.

8a. Sending an agreement through DocuSign

Live since 2026-09-17. An agreement can go out two ways, and the choice is made per job, on the job, not in Settings. Anthony’s reason: “if somebody is old school and we need to just send them a PDF to sign, we can do that — but we don’t need to go into the app and change the settings overall.”

DocuSign (the default, firm-wide)Send as a PDF
