The citations are chapter 736, §736.08135 and §736.0813. Who signs is a successor trustee receiving it for the trust’s records, never a representative adopting a court filing. And a block on its face states it supports the accounting and is not one — naming what a §736.08135 accounting contains that this does not (receipts, disbursements, gains and losses on sale, the identification of beneficiaries), because a list is something a reader can check and a bare disclaimer is a sentence they skim. ⚠ The NUMBER is deliberately the same. Date-of-death FMV is the §1014 stepped-up basis, the Form 706 figure and the carrying value a trust’s first accounting opens with, and the step-up reaches revocable trust assets. Do not add a trust valuation date.
⚠ Draft for counsel. The §736.08135 carrying-value mechanics were read through secondary summaries — the egress proxy blocked the primary sources during the spec pass — so this document’s operative wording and the agreement’s §5.2 trust arm go to the attorney with the rest of the agreement language before a real trust matter signs.
⚠ Every inventory document is named now, and none of them was. Chrome names a Save-as-PDF after the page title, and not one of these seven printers set one — so the Court Inventory filed with a probate court, the asset schedule sent to counsel and the release approval a personal representative signs all arrived in somebody’s downloads folder called “Havellin Palm Beach — Job Manager”. They read Havellin Court Inventory - 69 Beach Blvd - Sep 20 2026 and so on. Nothing else changed; if you have documents on file under the old name, they are the same documents.
All inventory valuations are documentation support, not a legal or appraisal opinion — the estate attorney and a credentialed appraiser remain the authority on any estate.

Where the inventory manifest actually lives

Until 2026-08-24 it lived only in one browser’s localStorage. The workbook write was one-way and nothing read back, so two devices held two different manifests of the same estate and the last sync overwrote the other wholesale; clearing site data destroyed the record. It now persists to a MediaStore blob in the main spreadsheet, beside jobs and estimates, via saveMedia / loadMedia.

It is deliberately NOT in the job’s Drive folder. Share w/ Counsel grants read-only access to the whole Estate Inventory folder, and the manifest carries custody logs, appraisal-waiver reasons and upload state — none of which counsel has any business reading. The workbook still goes to Drive: it is the manifest’s display columns and no internals. A test asserts saveMediaStore never touches DriveApp.
The merge is per ITEM, not per job, and must stay that way. _mergeStoreByKey — the helper the estimate store still uses — keeps whichever whole entry is newer. For an inventory that means two people editing different items on the same job still clobber each other. Every mutation stamps updatedAt and the merge resolves item by item against it. Removal writes a deletedAt tombstone rather than deleting the row, because absence is indistinguishable from “this device has not seen it yet” and a union merge would resurrect every deletion.
Consequence worth knowing: a tombstoned item still holds its item number, and the number is never reissued. A gap in the numbering is the visible record that something was removed.
A removal survives a stale device (checked 2026-09-30, with Remove line). A removal is stamped on the line’s clock, so a device that has not seen it cannot bring the line back by syncing its older copy, in the app or on the sheet (both merges keep the newer record, and a test drives both). Removed items says when each line was removed and, for a removal made at the desk, by whom. What it cannot survive is an edit: a device that changes the removed line afterwards, never having seen the removal, holds the newer record and brings the line back, which is the manifest’s rule for every field. Restore gives a line back under its own number, or the next free one if another device has since taken it. A row marked photo in the Drive bin was deleted in the field, and that mark survives a reload now (until 2026-09-30 it did not); any other row kept its photograph.
⚠ A busy device runs out of room, and the manifest is what fills it (2026-09-21). The manifest is the largest thing this app keeps on a device: measured, about 476 bytes a line when it is first captured and 791 once it is named and valued — so a 1,200-line estate is roughly 0.9MB and a 3,000-line one 2.5MB, against a browser allowance of about 5MB that the thumbnail cache already shares. A job’s manifest is only removed from a device when the client is deleted, never when the job finishes, so a laptop carries every estate it has ever worked and the third one is where it lands. One photograph carrying many lines gets it there faster. Until this date the write simply failed: the lines were on screen, nothing saved, one warning, and a reload had none of them. It now clears what the sheet can hand back and writes again — other jobs’ thumbnails first, then other jobs’ manifests — and tells you: cleared N KB of other jobs off this device. What that means for you: opening one of those older jobs pulls its inventory back down from the sheet, so you need a connection; offline it will read empty until you have one. Nothing was lost. It never touches the job you have open, never the held bytes of a photograph that failed to upload, and nothing at all on a device with no Apps Script URL configured — there the device is the only copy there is.

Release approval — written, itemised, before anything leaves

The deliverables page commits to “written approval requests, itemized item by item, before anything of value leaves the property. Verbal approval is never accepted.” Until 2026-09-01 the app recorded the result of an approval — Authorized By and Approval Date on the item — and had nothing that produced the request. It does now.

  • Approval Request on the working header lists everything awaiting approval; the same button on the bulk bar lists just what you have selected. Since 2026-10-03 (P19) awaiting means not yet approved by every fiduciary on the estate — the representative and every co-representative recorded on the client (a co-executor, a co-trustee). The request is addressed to all of them by name, prints a signature line for each, and where there is more than one says every one must sign. A line one of them has already signed for comes back on the next request, saying Signed so far by …; still to sign: …; since 2026-10-05 (P20) each signer with the day they signed (Signed so far by Ruth Adler (Oct 1, 2026); still to sign: Daniel Adler), and a line that has already left is listed apart, for ratification (below). With nobody recorded it is addressed by the matter: the Personal Representative on a probate matter or one not yet answered, the Successor Trustee on a trust, the Personal Representative or Successor Trustee on a pour-over, the Authorized Representative where there is neither a court nor a trust. (Until 2026-10-03 a trust’s request with nobody named went to a Personal Representative.)
  • Each line carries its photo, reference number, room, quantity, estimated value, proposed disposition and channel, with an Initial box, and the document states plainly that nothing on it will be moved, sold, donated or disposed of until the request is returned signed (a request carrying only lines already gone says instead, since 2026-10-05, that they have left the property and are listed for signature).
  • Keep, Move and Hold are excluded by definition — nothing is being released (a Move goes to the client’s own new home), so there is nothing to ask permission for. So is anything already approved by every fiduciary (until 2026-10-03, anything carrying an approval date, whoever had signed). An item with no disposition yet is not a request either.
  • A firearm on the list prints its own note, and it was rewritten on 2026-09-13 because it had gone false. It used to close “Havellin does not transport them” over every firearm on the page — the page the representative signs. It now states the two routes: every firearm goes to a named licensed dealer; where the representative separately authorises it in writing, naming each firearm by serial and the receiving dealer, Havellin’s named principal may drive a non-NFA firearm to that dealer, with title staying with the estate (with the trust, on a trust matter, since 2026-10-03) and Havellin taking neither ownership nor any share of proceeds — which since 2026-10-03 every estate request says of all its property, not of firearms alone (Havellin’s people never buy, below); and anything flagged NFA is counted separately and collected by the dealer, because Havellin does not take custody of one under any authority. Since 2026-09-30 a firearm going to a named person carries its route on its own line (through Palm Beach Arms, a licensed dealer), and the note adds that such a firearm goes to the dealer as well and the dealer makes the transfer to the person; Havellin never hands a firearm to anyone.
  • A line flagged Specific Bequest or Disputed is badged in its own row and named, with its item number, in a notice above the table (new 2026-09-11). The line is still on the request — that decision is the representative’s, not ours — but they are told which lines they are being asked to initial and why it matters. Since 2026-10-03 (P19) a line matched to a bequest on the Beneficiaries & specific bequests card (below) carries its own caution, Designated items proposed to go elsewhere, whenever it is proposed to go anywhere but to the person the bequest names: its row is badged with the person (BEQUEST TO MARY SMITH) and the notice above the table names the line, the person and the proposal. A matched line going to that person carries no caution: that is the bequest being carried out. SPECIFIC BEQUEST is now the caution for a line flagged with no bequest on the list behind it, and it says the property is designated by the will or the trust (it said left by the will, which is not true of a trust).
  • So is a line that has not been valued yet — badged NOT YET APPRAISED on an estate (new 2026-09-11). It fires when the item is flagged for a specialist, no appraiser is linked and no waiver is recorded. Again the line stays on the request: releasing property before it is valued is a decision the representative can properly make. What they are now told is that the estate will have no independent record of what it was worth when it left, which on an estate filing a federal return is a figure reported under oath. It is an estate caution: since 2026-09-30 a living client’s request prints neither the badge nor the notice above the table (the notice printed there until then, over rows that carried no badge). On a living job ⚑ Appraise means the piece is going out to a specialist, which the Where it is going column already says, and there is no estate to keep a record for. Specific Bequest and Disputed still print on both.
  • When the signed copy comes back, select those items and press Record approval. Since 2026-10-03 (P19) it opens a dialog rather than asking two questions: a tick for each fiduciary on the client, and the date. Since 2026-10-05 (P20, Q24) it records whoever is ticked, even when not every fiduciary has signed yet, and never refuses for it (until then it refused, naming who was missing, so a co-trustee who signed a week before the other could only be typed into the line’s record). Before you press it the dialog says what will stay open, as the ticks change (Daniel Adler has not signed yet: these lines stay on the next request until they do.), and it says it again once recorded; a co-trustee recorded while the dialog was open is named as still to sign. Each tick says who has already signed the selected lines (already signed all 2 lines). Every name ticked is written across every selected line in a single action, added to whoever the line records already: a name is never dropped, and one recorded twice keeps its first date. Each signer keeps their own day. Signed the same day, Authorized By reads Ruth Adler; Daniel Adler, as before; a week apart, it reads Ruth Adler (2026-10-01); Daniel Adler (2026-10-08), Approval Date is the later day, and the line under the box in the line’s record reads Signed: Ruth Adler (Oct 1, 2026); Daniel Adler (Oct 8, 2026), with who is still to sign. Every document prints the signers that way, never the stored dates: the request, the Disposition Ledger, the package’s record, the client’s workbook and the CSV. The line is approved once every fiduciary has signed it. Nobody ticked is refused (Not recorded: tick the signatures on the returned request.), and so is a missing date. With nobody recorded (a living client) it asks who signed, as before. It then offers File the signed request: the PDF or a photograph of the signed pages, filed to the client’s Signed Records folder against the lines it covers (Signed papers on the desk, below). Typing a name into twenty rows by hand is how a signed approval ends up recorded against three of them.
  • A co-representative recorded after an approval makes it incomplete (new 2026-10-03). The line reads approval incomplete on its row (hover it for who), comes back on the next request, and the Job Plan’s Written release authority on every item leaving names who is still to approve. One answer decides it everywhere: the request, the firearm gates and the Appraisal Worklist, the desk and the Job Plan. Since 2026-10-05 (P20) a line that has already left reads ratification owed instead (next).
  • A line that left before every fiduciary approved it is listed apart, for ratification (new 2026-10-05, P20, Q23; Anthony: ratify, nothing is undone). A line leaving the property that is recorded as gone — a Date of Disposition, a Released event on its chain of custody, or a signed receipt filed for it (the same test the receipts read) — while its written approval is not complete (a co-trustee recorded after the hand-over, a signature still to come, or none at all) is not ready to be released: it left. The next Approval Request lists it after the lines asked for, under Already released: for ratification: “These items left the property before every co-trustee had approved their release in writing. They are listed apart from the items above, for the signature of Daniel Adler, which ratifies each release; nothing is undone.” (with one representative, in the role’s words: before the Personal Representative had approved). Each line there says when it went (Released Oct 2, 2026) and who has signed and who has not, under a Disposition column rather than a proposed one. A request carrying only such lines says so at the top and never that anything is ready to be released. The line’s row reads ratification owed, never approval incomplete, and the Job Plan’s Written release authority on every item leaving counts these apart (3 already left and await ratification by Ruth Adler and Daniel Adler (the request lists them apart)). Record approval records the missing signature like any other, partial or complete, and once every fiduciary has signed the line drops off. One answer decides it on all three. An estate only.
⚠ Until 2026-09-11 neither flag reached a single printed document. flagBequest and flagDisputed were editable on the manifest, exported to the attorney’s workbook and painted as chips on screen — and every print path ignored them. Measured on a real estate: a $48,000 painting the will leaves to a named person printed identically to a set of dining chairs, proposed for Auction, with an initial box beside it; the words “bequest” and “dispute” appeared nowhere in the document. Any release approval request printed before 2026-09-11 is worth re-reading against the manifest — it did not tell the representative what it was asking for. The same was true of an unvalued line: an object the app had itself flagged for a specialist, with no value on record, printed with Auction proposed and an initial box beside it, and the document never said it had not been valued. Measured on the same estate, the request went from 5,127 bytes to 6,292 once all three cautions printed.
It flags and explains; it never refuses, and never drops the line. Same rule as the firearms gate. A specific bequest can properly be sold — it may have adeemed, the beneficiary may have disclaimed, the proceeds may be needed for the administration — and that is the representative’s decision on counsel’s advice. Withholding the line would simply hide it. To take a line off a request instead, set its disposition to Keep or Hold, which excludes it by definition.
Two more surfaces carry the same marking, on purpose. The Disposition Ledger badges them too — that is the permanent record of where each item went (it was the Disposition & Accounting Ledger until 2026-10-03), and a bequest that was sold has to be findable in it, or nothing on the page shows the decision was taken deliberately. And the bulk bar names them in its confirmation: set Auction across forty rows and it tells you which of them are flagged, by item number (and, since 2026-10-03, the beneficiary of a matched line), at the moment you do it rather than when the request reaches the representative.

Beneficiaries and specific bequests (new 2026-10-03, P19)

Anthony, 2026-10-03, on the estate workflow (call 7): a beneficiary list, and a list of specific bequests that items are checked against. On an estate the desk carries a Beneficiaries & specific bequests card under the Inventory Review bar, with two lists that are the representative’s or counsel’s and never ours: the beneficiaries (name, relationship, phone and email; Save beneficiary, and Edit and Remove on each) and the specific bequests — each the description as it was given to us, the beneficiary it names, and the inventory lines matched to it. Havellin never reads or interprets the will or the trust: a bequest here is the list of designated items the representative or counsel hands over, in their words.

  • Lines are matched by item number: in Item #s when the bequest is saved, or in the bequest’s own Match box later. A number no line carries is refused by name, and so is a line already matched to another bequest (a line belongs to one: unmatch it there first, with its ✕). Matching a line marks it a specific bequest. Unmatching it, or removing the bequest, leaves the flag for a person to clear in the line’s record; while a line is matched, its flag cannot be cleared there.
  • Three checks, on the card. A line flagged Specific Bequest that no bequest on the list covers is named (Flagged Specific Bequest, and no bequest on the list covers it: #4 Tabriz rug). A bequest with no line matched reads not yet found. A matched line proposed to go anywhere but to the person its bequest names — sold, consigned, auctioned, donated, removed, or released to somebody else — is named with that person and the proposal (Designated items proposed to go elsewhere: #1 Sargent portrait (bequest to Mary Smith; proposed: Auction)). That last is the same caution the release approval request prints, the line’s row carries (⚠ bequest to Mary Smith · going elsewhere) and the bulk bar names when it sweeps a disposition across the line. It flags and explains; it never refuses.
  • The roster is offered wherever a recipient is typed for a line going To a person — the row’s box, the line’s Channel / Recipient and the bulk bar’s Who took them… — as suggestions. The box stays free text.
  • A beneficiary a bequest names is not removed: change or remove the bequest first. Both lists are the job’s record lists, saved entry by entry, so two devices adding to them at once keep both — on the Apps Script deployment of 2026-10-03; an older one can drop an entry another device added.

Receipts for property released to a person (new 2026-10-03, P19)

The estate agreement’s §5.3 asks for written approval “+ signed receipt from recipient” to move or deliver items to beneficiaries, and the client estimate promises that “a signed receipt is held for every item released to a beneficiary”. Until 2026-10-03 nothing held one: Receipt / Doc is a free-text box. On an estate the desk now prints the receipt and files the signed copy back.

  • What counts as released to a person: a line going To a person (Distribute) whose written approval is complete — every fiduciary’s — or which is recorded as gone: a Date of Disposition, a Released event on its chain of custody, or a signed receipt already filed for it. A line still waiting on written approval is not: it may not leave yet, so no receipt can be owed for it, and flagging every line as it is sorted would be a flag people learn to read past.
  • Releases & signed papers (the card under the Inventory Review bar) lists each recipient: what has been released to them, what has no signed receipt filed (⚠ no signed receipt filed for #2), and what is still waiting on approval. Print receipt prints one receipt for that person listing what is owed — or, once everything is receipted, everything released to them (Reprint receipt): item number, description, quantity and condition; the recipient’s acknowledgment; their signature, printed name and date; a witness line for Havellin; and who the property came from — the Estate of the decedent, the trustee of the trust (its name as recorded on the client), or either on a pour-over. File signed receipt (#2) files the PDF or photograph of the signed page to the client’s Signed Records folder against exactly the lines it names.
  • A line counts as receipted only when a signed receipt filed to Drive lists it, filed for the person it is released to now. A Receipt / Doc typed on the line is shown as what it is — a typed reference, not a signed receipt filed to Drive — and does not clear it. A filed receipt voided as filed against the wrong paper stops counting, and a line re-routed to somebody else after one person’s receipt was filed is owed the new person’s (the first receipt stays in Drive and on the record).
  • A line released with no signed receipt filed reads no signed receipt on its row and is counted by the No signed receipt chip in Needs you, and Job Admin carries Signed receipt for every item released to a person with N of M. It flags; it never refuses a release.
  • The receipt is an estate paper. The living-client agreement has no receipt requirement (its §10.1b says so), and the desk offers none.
⚠ The receipt’s statement is drafted, not reviewed. It records a delivery: “I acknowledge that I received the property listed above, in the condition described, from [the Estate of … / the trustee of …], delivered to me by Havellin Palm Beach, LLC as directed in writing by [the fiduciaries]. This receipt records the delivery only: it does not decide who owns the property or what it is worth, and it does not release or waive any right or claim concerning [the estate / the trust].” A beneficiary’s receipt-and-release is counsel’s paper, never ours, and the wording goes to counsel with the rest.

Signed papers on the desk (new 2026-10-03, P19)

The Releases & signed papers card files each signed paper to the client’s Signed Records folder (the job’s own folder, on a Drive folder made before 2026-10-03) beside what it covers, and lists the copies filed: each a link with who signed and when, and a Void for one filed against the wrong paper. A voided copy stays in Drive and on the record; nothing here deletes one.

  • Signed release approvals — since 2026-10-05 (P20) each signing act: the day, who signed that day and the lines they signed, with File signed copy, and Signed request not filed to Drive yet until one is. When one co-trustee signs a week before the other, each signature is its own row with its own filed copy, and the first stays listed after the second completes the lines (until 2026-10-05 the second signature rewrote the first approval, and the copy filed for it dropped off the card). A copy filed for a day no line records any more (a line removed, an approval cleared by hand) is still listed, saying so. An act whose lines are still open says who has not approved them and that they go back on the next approval request, and which of them, already gone, go apart, for ratification.
  • Receipts — above.
  • Signed schedules — Court Inventory, once the representative has adopted it on a probate matter (or one not yet answered), Trust Schedule, once the trustee has signed it as received on a trust, both on a pour-over, and neither on Neither or where the engagement states no values: File the adopted copy and File the received copy.

The other signed papers file the same way, from their own cards: the Disposition Ledger, the charity receipts and the proceeds statements (Close-out records), and the will and cash receipts (the Job Plan’s Found on site card, §11). A living client’s card carries its approvals alone.

Havellin’s people never buy or receive estate property (new 2026-10-03, P19)

Anthony, 2026-10-03 (call 6): the firearms rule extended to all estate property, because anything else is a conflict of interest. On an estate, a sale (Auction, Consign, Sell) or a release To a person whose Channel / Recipient names one of Havellin’s people — anyone in the contractor directory, active or not (the concierges and the crew), a manager, or the job’s own concierge, compared as people, so Ashley Jerome (concierge) is Ashley Jerome — is refused where it is written: the row’s recipient box and disposition, the line’s record, and the bulk bar, which refuses the whole sweep and names every line. The refusal names the rule: Havellin and its people never buy or receive estate property, and take no share of the proceeds. Ashley Jerome is with Havellin, so #1 Sargent portrait cannot be sold or released to them. Nothing was changed. (trust property on a trust). A donation or a haul-away is outside it. A living client is not refused: an owner gives their own things to whom they like. Every estate release approval request says it as well: Havellin and its people never purchase or receive estate property, and take no share of the proceeds of its sale.

On a living client’s job it is flagged, never refused (new 2026-10-05, P20, Q25; Anthony: flag it there, never refuse). Until 2026-10-05 nothing there said so at all. The same answer decides who counts as one of Havellin’s people on every job, and on a living client’s a sale or a release To a person naming one of them is written and put on the record, so the client signs knowing: the row reads ⚠ Ashley Jerome works with Havellin, the line’s record says why it is flagged and not refused, the bulk bar’s confirmation names the line and the person (for a disposition or a recipient set across a selection), and the release approval request prints Property going to someone who works with Havellin above the table, naming each line and the person, with GOING TO ASHLEY JEROME, WHO WORKS WITH HAVELLIN on its row: “These lines are proposed to be sold or given to a person who works with Havellin, named against each line. What happens to your own property is your decision, and you may sell it or give it to whomever you choose; because the person works with us, it is a conflict of interest for Havellin, so we put it on the record before anything leaves. Initialling a line below confirms that you know who is receiving it. To send a line somewhere else instead, ask your concierge.” The Contents Record and the Disposition Ledger badge the line too. An estate never prints it: there the write is refused, as above.

Photographs on the manifest

Every row shows a thumbnail of its item photo, and the Estate Inventory Report and the Approval Request print them. They are fetched through the Apps Script, which runs as the Havellin Google account and already holds Drive access, and cached per job on the device.

They are deliberately NOT loaded from drive.google.com directly, and must not be. Share w/ Counsel grants access with named viewers only — never “anyone with the link” — so Drive demands an authenticated session, and the app is served from GitHub Pages, which makes that a cross-site request. Safari blocks third-party cookies by default. A direct image link would render on desktop Chrome and fail on the iPad, and failing on one device only is worse than failing on all of them: it reads as missing photos on exactly the machine the work is done on. There is a test asserting no such URL survives in code.
Every upload has always returned a Drive file id and the app discarded it until 2026-09-01. It is stored now, and for photos taken before then it is recovered out of the URL that was stored — so nothing in the existing backlog is stranded. A photo that still cannot be resolved renders a category glyph, never a broken image; the report says how many are missing rather than printing empty boxes at a client.
⚠ It needs a deployment from 2026-09-01 or later. main-sync.gs gained a getThumbnails action on 2026-09-01. On an older one the tab works normally but every photo shows a glyph — and says why, rather than leaving grey squares unexplained.

Firearms — the authority gate, the transport gate, and the NFA flag

A firearm is the one category where the app deliberately refuses to produce a document. Nothing reaches the Appraisal Worklist until the representative has authorised the transfer to a named licensed dealer, in writing — and since 2026-10-03 (P19), every co-representative; the worklist names who by the matter (the Personal Representative, the Successor Trustee, every co-trustee). Before 2026-08-24 it did not: flagged firearms were grouped under a Firearms Specialist (FFL) and a handover packet printed on day one, which is a printed instruction to release a firearm nobody had authorised releasing.

Authority is recorded on the item itself, using columns that already existed — Authorized By and Approval Date, with the dealer named in Channel / Recipient. There is no separate firearms screen; the one field added since is Serial Number (2026-09-13), which the transport gate reads, and one record: a firearm going to a named person carries its dealer route (2026-09-30), below.

Authority and custody are different questions, and the app keeps them apart. Authority — who decides what happens — belongs to the representative on counsel’s advice; that is what routing a firearm “to the attorney” means, routing the decision. Custody — who may lawfully possess and move it — was the dealer’s alone until 2026-09-13, and for an NFA item it still is. For an ordinary firearm it is now shared: see the transport gate below.
The awaiting-authority notice is NOT filtered through the appraisal test, and this is deliberate. invNeedsAppraisal answers a valuation question — an intrinsic category with no value yet, or a value at or above $3,000. A $900 shotgun with a value recorded against it is therefore never on the worklist at all, and an earlier version of this feature consequently reported it nowhere. The cheap, ordinary firearm is the one most likely to be picked up without a second thought. Do not re-narrow this filter.

The transport gate — what may go in the car (new 2026-09-13)

The standing rule until this date was that Havellin never transports a firearm and the dealer collects from the property. That was one rule written over two very different questions, and it was too broad. For a non-NFA firearm the pieces line up: Fla. Stat. § 790.25(5) covers a securely-encased firearm in a private conveyance, § 733.607 puts possession and control in the personal representative’s hands with a duty to take all steps reasonably necessary to preserve the estate, and ATF treats temporary custody where title and ultimate control stay with the owner as not a transfer. The destination is an FFL, which is the most protected recipient in the statute. That is what estate liquidators do. For an NFA item it is not close, and the app refuses outright.

So the rule is now a gate on the item, invTransportBlocked, with five arms answered in this order. The first two are different from the other three: no written authority clears either. The NFA arm never clears; the beneficiary arm clears only when the line records its dealer route (since 2026-09-30).

ArmWhat it meansHow it clears
NFAThe item is ticked NFA Item. It is never carried by Havellin, on any job, under any authority.It does not clear. The dealer collects it.
BeneficiaryThe disposition is Distribute: the firearm is going to a named person (added 2026-09-21), and the line records no dealer route. Nobody hands a firearm over directly, family included; the transfer runs through a licensed dealer, who does the background check and the paperwork.Not by authority: by the route (since 2026-09-30). Open the line (▾) and press Through a licensed dealer… in its Firearm block, naming the dealer. The person stays in Channel / Recipient. Until then nothing cleared it while the line read Distribute.
AuthorityNo written authority on file — or, since 2026-10-03, an approval that does not name every fiduciary on the client (Written authority is not complete. Daniel Adler has not approved it, and every co-trustee must).Record Authorized By and Approval Date for every fiduciary: Record approval writes them (since 2026-10-05 as each signs, each on their own day; the firearm stays held until all have). Both, or neither counts.
SerialNo serial number recorded.Fill in Serial Number on the row.
DealerNo receiving dealer named.Name the dealer in Channel / Recipient. On a line going to a named person the dealer is the route’s, because Channel / Recipient holds the person there.
To a person, through a licensed dealer (new 2026-09-30). Anthony, asked whether a firearm left to a family member should have a way through: “sure”. Until this date the beneficiary arm refused anything marked Distribute and nothing cleared it — no disposition means to a family member through a dealer — so a firearm left to a daughter could never be driven to the dealer who would transfer it to her. Now the line’s Firearm block offers Through a licensed dealer… on a non-NFA firearm going to a person. It asks for the dealer and records who and when (the concierge assigned to the job, as the desk’s other ticks do), and the firearm then clears on the same terms as any other going to a dealer: written authority naming it by serial, the serial on the record, the dealer named — and Anthony alone carries it. The person stays in Channel / Recipient, because the line still records who ends up with it. The row reads via the dealer (or no dealer route in bronze), the Approval Request prints through …, a licensed dealer on the line and says how a firearm reaches a person, and the worklist names the route on the cleared line. It is offered on nothing else — never on an NFA item — and the handler refuses what the control withholds. Take the route off clears it, stamped so another device cannot put it back; the route survives a newer edit from a device that never saw it, as the written authority does.
⚠⚠ The NFA arm has no override, and must never grow one. It is one of the few refusals in the app nobody can override — the others are the deposit gate on hours logging and a final invoice with no hours logged, and until 2026-09-30 the beneficiary arm above, which now clears by its route — and for the same reason: there is no person with standing to unlock it. A representative cannot authorise it because the registration is federal and not theirs to assign. 27 CFR § 479.11 defines transfer as “selling, assigning, pledging, leasing, loaning, giving away, or otherwise disposing of”, and its only temporary-conveyance exception reaches a qualified manufacturer or dealer, for the sole purpose of repair, identification, evaluation, research, testing or calibration, and return to the same lawful possessor — three separate misses. § 479.90a’s estate carve-out names the executor, administrator or personal representative: a fiduciary category, not a vendor they engage. 26 U.S.C. § 5861 is ten years. A test drives a fully-papered NFA item — authority, serial, dealer, everything — and fails if it is ever anything but blocked.
The serial is a gate rather than a nicety. The authorisation names each firearm by serial, so an item with none cannot have been named in the document that makes carrying it lawful — and the dealer’s receipt comes back serial by serial and has to reconcile against the manifest line for line. Twelve in the case and eleven on the receipt is a problem nobody can unwind six months later.
Transport is stricter than release, and it is not a rename of it. invReleaseBlocked asks whether the item may leave the property at all; the transport gate asks who carries it. An authorised NFA item is perfectly releasable to the dealer and is never transportable by us. The Appraisal Worklist prints both answers: how many firearms are cleared to carry, with their serials, and each blocked one with the reason and the fix.
⚠ The risk that matters is not the drive, it is the fee. 18 U.S.C. § 922(a)(1)(A) — engaging in the business of dealing without a licence — is keyed to purchase and resale, so a courier paid its logged hours is clear. Two things would cross it and both are now written policy: never buy an estate firearm, and never take a percentage of firearm proceeds. Havellin bills hours, so it is structurally fine today; the hazard is somebody later “simplifying” firearms into a commission the way prep carries 30%. (BSCA 2022 broadened the test from “livelihood and profit” to “predominantly earn a profit”; ATF’s 2024 presumptions are proposed for rescission under RIN 1140-AB01, but the statutory definition stands either way.)
⚠ One named principal carries, and the app cannot enforce that. Policy is that Anthony transports and nobody else — not Ashley, not a specialist, not a 1099 contractor — and the app has no concept of who is driving. That is a document rather than a gate today, which is the honest state. If this ever becomes a service line rather than something one person does, the crew screening question (§ 922(g), Fla. Stat. § 790.23) needs a real answer before the rule can be widened.

The NFA flag — what it is now

NFA Item is a tick box on the inventory row, offered only where the category is Firearms. From 2026-08-24 to 2026-09-13 it gated nothing — it printed a worklist notice and decided no behaviour anywhere. It is load-bearing now: it is the first arm of the transport gate, and the only one that cannot be cleared. Which is also why the flag’s third reason was always the real one:

  • The dealer needs telling in advance. Not every licensed dealer may take an NFA item, so an unannounced one can mean a wasted collection trip.
  • It is a closing-timeline item — but a much smaller one than it was. Corrected 2026-09-13: this section used to say a Form 5 transfer to an estate “takes months, not days”, and the worklist printed the same. Form 5 eForms clear in about two days in 2026 (paper about nine), so that sentence was putting a closing-timeline worry in front of the representative that no longer exists. A Form 4 to a dealer is the slow one; do not promise either timeline to a client.
  • Recognition — and this is the one that matters. A suppressor reads as a plain metal tube and a short-barrelled rifle as a small gun. The risk was never mishandling one; it is never categorising it as a firearm at all, in which case every gate downstream stays shut and it rides in the trunk anyway. That is why the crew checklist and the protocol name these items by appearance rather than by law.

NFA-flagged items print on their own notice on the Appraisal Worklist, separate from the awaiting-authority list, now stating that Havellin does not carry one under any authority and that it transfers from the estate to the dealer on ATF paperwork. An item appears there whether or not it has been authorised — the dealer needs telling regardless.

The protocol document

firearms-protocol.html sits in the repository beside this manual and the playbook and is served from the same Pages site. It is the live procedure: six stages from the intake call to closing the record, the NFA recognition check, seven nevers, the transport kit, the client-facing claim language, and three questions for counsel. It is linked from four places in the app, each a relative path opening in a new tab: the red firearms banner at the top of the Job Plan, the firearms line pinned in the room workspace, the firearms row of the standing-flags brief on the Client Dashboard (the Job Plan’s own brief stopped repeating that row on 2026-09-20, because the banner above it is the same row), and the transport block on the Appraisal Worklist. Deliberately not the Drive copy and not a hosted preview, because it is opened on a phone standing in somebody’s house at the moment a firearm turns up and neither of those resolves without a sign-in and a good signal.

⚠ Three copies exist and only one is the procedure. The repository page is what the app links to and what the crew opens. The Google Doc in 03_Operations / SOPs & Playbooks is counsel’s review copy — a point-in-time draft to be redlined; when counsel signs off, the repository page is updated and the Doc is marked superseded. Anything else is a drafting preview and is not authoritative. It is a draft for counsel review and says so on its face: several primary sources were unreachable during drafting and were read through secondary summaries, so the operative text needs verifying before it is adopted.
Still open and flagged for counsel: the app has no view on which dealer may take an NFA item, and does not attempt one. It reports; the decision is the representative’s and the dealer’s.

11. Job Plan & Log Hours

Tab: Job Plan opens on the client you last opened (or pick one from its menu). The plan appears once the estimate is approved and the client’s acceptance is recorded; until then the tab says which is missing. For labor-based services it runs in the order a concierge works a job (rebuilt 2026-09-19, evening; the folds came off the job itself on 2026-09-20): the header with its schedule strip (§9a-i), the brown Next band (below), a row of gate chips, the two tools folded at the top — Vendors & partners and Hours & daily close — and then the job, open, on one thread: Before Day 1, In the house (the room cards and the room workspace, §10), Midpoint & pickups, Move day on Home Transition, and Close-out. Above the chips sit, when they apply, the red All rooms are locked — you’re at the project midpoint banner (while the midpoint invoice is unsent and the job is not closed) and a note of the walkthrough items not yet brought into the inventory (§10a). For Home Prep for Sale it is the streamlined vendor/budget/checklist view described in Section 6c.

The stages, in job order (rebuilt 2026-09-19, evening). Anthony, reading the plan the field-capture build shipped that afternoon: “the rooms come before the phase zero pre-job authority, so that seems out of order … vendor and partner sourcing should be at the top before we get to the rooms, because we’re going to make these calls and line these people up before the job even starts … there’s only phase two and phase four, there’s no phase three … apply some human logic to how we would run a job.” He was right on every point. The order now:

⚠ THE COURT CHIPS FOLLOW THE MATTER, AND THE ATTORNEY IS ASKED FOR ON A PROBATE MATTER (2026-09-29, workflow audit M5). Letters of Administration on file and §733.604 inventory deadline render on any estate whose matter is administered through probate — an Estate Settlement included — and on a Probate service whose matter is unanswered, as before. They keyed on the service, so an Estate Settlement on a probate estate was never asked for either, on the screen that would have caught it. Estate attorney on file is asked on a probate matter, where Florida requires the personal representative to be represented, and is shown green anywhere an attorney is recorded. It used to sit red on a trust administration with no counsel of record, where a successor trustee often acts alone — a line nobody can close is the one people learn to read past. A red Letters chip now also says the job cannot activate without them. Since 2026-10-03 the chip names the paper the matter takes (P19): Letters of Administration on file on the probate track, Both included; Certification of Trust on file on a trust-only matter — the successor trustee’s proof of authority (Fla. Stat. §736.1017), which a trust job waits on to activate as a probate job waits on the Letters; and nothing on Neither or on an Estate Settlement whose matter is unanswered. It reads the same answer the activation gate reads, recorded in the same place on Edit Client.
The Form 706 chip (new 2026-10-03, P19). Anthony: track the federal estate tax return’s due date where it applies. On an estate whose 706 is not answered No, the chip row carries Form 706 due <date>: nine months after the date of death, on the last day of the month where that month is shorter (31 May gives 28 February). Green once the 706 is answered Yes and the day is still ahead: a federal deadline Havellin keeps in view but neither files nor can clear, and a red chip nobody here can close for nine months is the line people learn to read past. Red while the 706 is unanswered, labelled …, if one is filed, with the fix to ask the estate attorney and answer Form 706 being filed? under Edit Client (the same answer sets the documentation standard); and red once the day has passed, saying to confirm with the estate attorney that the return, or an extension on Form 4768, went in. Havellin never sees the filing and the chip never claims it: counsel or the accountant files the return. An estate whose 706 is answered No has no chip.
The Close-out card — every service, on the Job Plan and on Job Admin & Inv (new 2026-09-22). Anthony: “should we rate every vendor we use at the end of a job? … shouldn’t we close out with asking for a client review on google or something? job closeout needs work too.” The client half of the close was three boxes at the foot of the Close-out stage, and a Home Prep job had none of it. It is one card now, four numbered steps in the order they are done:
  1. Satisfaction call — a call, not a text. Tick it once the client has told you they are happy.
  2. Google review — Draft review request writes a short thank-you with the review link (g.page/r/CcQOjVMdHUcnEBM/review) into your own Gmail, the same way invoices go: you read it, you send it, then tap I’ve sent it. Once it is posted, tick Review posted on Google. The button is greyed out until the satisfaction call is ticked — a review request that reaches a client before anyone has checked they are happy is how a firm collects its one-star reviews. On an estate the email goes to the representative and says the property, never your home. If Gmail is not set up on the device it opens a plain email instead.
  3. Referral ask — tick it once asked.
  4. Vendor scorecard — one to five stars on every vendor used, with an optional note. It now lists all four kinds — service vendors, collection partners, prep trades and the end-of-job haulers (the last two were never on it, so a prep job read No vendors were assigned over four confirmed trades). Used means Confirmed on the sourcing card (a collection partner, which has no status there, once it is assigned); a firm that only quoted is not listed. A firm on two lines is one row. Each star saves itself to the job and to the Vendor Directory’s rolling score — the old Write scores to vendor directory button is gone — and each row says whether it reached the directory.
⚠ Ratings are required. Close job refuses while any vendor used is unrated, and names who is outstanding. It gates the close only — nothing mid-job asks for a verdict on work still in progress. Since 2026-09-29 Close job is offered from the day the job is active (§9a), so the scorecard is the one thing to finish before pressing it. A close made by mistake is undone with Re-open job until the final invoice goes out or a final payment is recorded (§9). The call and referral ticks keep the keys they had on the Close-out stage, so a tick made before today still reads ticked.
The brown Next band is on the Job Plan too (new 2026-09-22), under the whole header (the firearms banner, the client line with its schedule strip, and the standing-flags brief) and above the plan, with the Deposit & Start half of the timeline beneath it — the Client Dashboard’s own band and buttons. Send the midpoint invoice, record a payment or close the job from here without leaving the plan. It appears once the job is won.
⚠⚠ THE PER-JOB NDA BOX IS GONE, AND SO IS THE CONTRACT CLAUSE IT SERVED (2026-09-20). NDA signed by every crew member and contractor before entry was a Before Day 1 box on every labour job. Anthony: “we do not require NDAs for staff on all jobs. confidentiality is baked into our 1099 employment agreements.” So it asked a person to attest to a signature nobody takes. The half that matters is the document it came from: the estate and probate agreement’s §7 promised the personal representative that Havellin would “require all team members and contractors to sign a Non-Disclosure Agreement before accessing the property” — a written commitment, on the matters where the contents are most sensitive, that the firm does not perform. §7 states the real mechanism now: every team member and contractor is engaged under a written agreement carrying confidentiality obligations, executed before they work on any Havellin engagement. The duty is unchanged in substance and standing rather than per-job. The living-client agreement never made the NDA promise and is untouched. Every labour service therefore carries one box fewer under Before Day 1 — measured on the real renderer after the removal that day, the counts were Home Editing 8, Home Transition 27, Home Cleanout 12, Estate Settlement 16, Probate and Contested Probate 17 (Home Prep for Sale, a different renderer, was unchanged at 6); the table under What each service type’s plan actually contains, below, carries today’s. ⚠ Do not restore the box without the process behind it: a clause the firm does not perform is the same defect as the 15% vendor fee, the licensed claim and cost-plus materials, every one of them found by reading a document rather than the code.
The phase numbers are gone, and why there was never a Phase 3 on most jobs. Phase 3 was Move Day, which only Home Transition has, so every other service counted 0, 1, 2, 4 and read as a form with a page missing. The stages are named now. The task keys underneath are unchanged, so every tick made on the named boxes since 2026-09-19 carries.
The gates are chips, not a section. Agreement signed, deposit received and Job team confirmed on every labour job; vendors lined up once the job carries a vendor (the estimate’s, or an end-of-job line added on the plan); and, on a matter administered through probate, the Letters, the estate attorney and the §733.604 deadline, with an attorney recorded on any other estate shown as a green chip (2026-09-29, above). Green or red, read off the record, nothing to tick. A red chip prints its fix under the row (Deposit received: not yet — no crew is scheduled before it lands), because the iPad this tab is worked from cannot hover a tooltip.
⚠ A Job team confirmed chip, on every labour job (new 2026-09-23). Anthony: “the timeline really only reflects the sort of invoicing and financial stuff. And now that we’re in the job plan, we need notifications up there that are job specific.” The team sign-off was the one job-side gate with no chip: it lives at the foot of the Hours & daily close fold, which is shut until somebody opens it, so an unconfirmed team said nothing anywhere on the page. The chip is red until the team is confirmed, and red again if one person holds two slots — a team confirmed with Anthony Jr in both specialist slots is not a confirmed team. Its fix names the button, and it turns green the moment Save & Confirm Job Team goes through, with no redraw.
The hours log is a tool at the top of the plan, folded (moved there 2026-09-20). The entry form, the summary against the estimate, the forward projection and the history are one fold, Hours & daily close, directly under Vendors & partners and above the job — it spent one evening (2026-09-19) folded under the rooms. It never opens by itself; the person logging the day opens it. Its fold reads today 3 hrs · 36 of 63 logged; on an active job with nothing logged for today it reads no hours logged today in amber, so a closed card cannot hide a day that was never written down. Nothing about logging changed — the same form, the same rows, the same projection. Two things on it were corrected later the same day, both found by driving the real form on a fixed-price job: the count on the fold and the schedule strip on the header now move the moment an entry saves or a line is voided (until then both sat on the last redraw, so the fold went on reading no hours logged today in amber over the entry that had just cleared it), and the specialist selects offer Contractor TBD from the first draw (a rebuild that runs on every plan open used to overwrite them with None / … / Other, so the placeholder the team sign-off tells you to pick was not on the list until the form had been redrawn once — and Other, if picked, was stored as a crew member named Other).
The job body does not fold, and the stage you are in is marked, not opened (2026-09-20). Anthony, off a screenshot of three stages behind three dark bars: “not sure this needs to be condensed with expanders … it’s not that much info … let’s rethink this all to make more sense to a human doing a job … Maybe the vendors and partners and hours in daily close are at the top and both expandable. But everything else just sort of runs in the order that it’s done in a job … the main body should just be the job you’re working and everything should flow logically through that.” So Before Day 1, In the house, Midpoint & pickups, Move day and Close-out are open cards on one thread with a node each: green behind you, bronze NOW where the job is, grey ahead. Where the job is: Before Day 1 until it is active, In the house while it is, Midpoint & pickups once every room is locked, Close-out once the midpoint is paid and every room is cleared — or the moment the job is closed, whatever the midpoint says (2026-09-29). Move day is never marked NOW — it reads done once Close-out does. The only fold that opens by itself is Vendors & partners, while the job is not yet active and a line is unconfirmed; the hours fold waits to be tapped. Anything you open or close yourself stays that way for the session, and a redraw (a photo landing from the other device) never touches it.
The boxes are sentences, and a lone box spans the row (2026-09-20). Every checkbox label used to render through the form-label rule — ten-point capitals, letter-spaced — so a stage of thirteen boxes read as a wall of shouting. They are sentence case at a readable size now, three across on a desk, two on the iPad, one on a phone, and a ticked box greys and strikes through. A section holding one box (the pre-job call) spans the whole row instead of wrapping to four lines in a third of the width; Anthony: “for this item, it should stretch all the way across.”
The room cards run in walkthrough order — Entry & Living, then Kitchen & Utility, then Lifestyle Rooms, and so on: the same top-to-bottom as the Build Estimate grid and the client estimate, so a room can be found in one document by its position in the other. It is drawn once (rebuilt 2026-09-19, §10); the two phase grids are gone. A room saved without a grid position keeps its stored place at the end rather than dropping out of the plan.
⚠ A firearms flag at intake is a red banner across the top of the Job Plan (new 2026-09-19) — FIREARMS IN THIS HOUSE — flagged at intake, the standing rule, what intake wrote down (or Ticked at intake, no detail recorded — ask the client before Day 1) and the protocol link. It is the first element in the header, above the client line and above the standing-flags brief — which, since 2026-09-20, does not repeat the firearms row: the banner is that row, so a job whose only flag is firearms shows the banner and no brief at all, while the client dashboard, which has no banner, keeps the red row — and a slimmer line of the same sits at the top of every room’s workspace on that job. Nothing renders on a job intake did not flag. It also keeps the two firearms boxes under the room grid (Before anything leaves the house) for that job, whatever the service type. The Home Prep plan opens with the same banner when the record carries the flag (since 2026-09-20). Home Prep intake does not ask the firearms question (§4), so on a prep job that is a flag recorded before the service was switched to Home Prep, or by an older intake.
Standing job flags sit at the top of the plan (new 2026-09-10). Whatever intake recorded on the What’s in the house questions (§4) renders here as a banded panel — the must-find and safety answers first, then the rest (firearms is not in it on the plan: since 2026-09-20 it is the red banner above, not a row). It is part of the plan header, so it is the first thing on screen when the tab opens. It renders on the Home Prep plan too, where intake asks only the safety question and the Access & security row (since 2026-09-30, Q17; §4): vendors go through the house, so the alarm, the keys and who else comes and goes matter there, and nothing leaves it, so the rest is not asked. A job with nothing recorded shows no panel. The banner — and, on the client dashboard, the red firearms row — carries a Read the firearms protocol → link (new 2026-09-13), opening the full procedure in a new tab so a tap never navigates off the plan the crew is working from. The document rides on the flag’s catalogue entry rather than on the renderer, so any other flag can carry a protocol the same way and no render site ever learns the word firearms.
Must-finds carry a Found it tick (new 2026-09-19), and the brief is on four screens. Each line of the must-find answer is its own row with a Found it button. Pressing it turns the row green, drops it below the lines still missing and prints ✓ Found Sep 19, 2026 · Ashley Jerome — the date, and the concierge assigned to the job at the moment of the tick. The app has no sign-in; the assigned concierge is who is accountable for the brief, and the name is frozen at the tick so a later reassignment does not rewrite who found what. Undo takes it back. The tick is stored on the job record and saved like every other job edit, so it shows on the Job Plan header, on the client dashboard, in every room’s workspace (§10) and above Job Admin on the Job Admin & Inv tab (§10a), and whichever of those is open repaints the moment it is pressed. The tick is keyed to the line’s wording: reword a must-find on Edit Client after it is found and its tick clears rather than moving onto words it was not made for. Two devices: the tick merges per key on the sheet (from Apps Script 2026-09-19a), so one made in the house survives an unrelated edit of the same client on a stale laptop, and an untick is a real removal rather than an absence the laptop puts back. The intake flags beside it merge the same way, row by row, from the 2026-09-30 redeploy (§2); until then a flag goes with the newer whole record. A tick made on the other device shows here after a reload; nothing polls the client record mid-job.
⚠⚠ Print Job Plan IS GONE (retired 2026-09-11). The button is off the tab and there is no printed plan any more. It was fixed that morning — it had been printing the phase headings and nothing under them — and printing it in full is exactly what showed it was not worth having: an 18-room probate plan came out at 9 Letter pages, carrying 36 room cards for 18 rooms (every room rendered twice on that build, once in the Phase 1 sort grid and again in the Phase 2 pack grid) and 72 checkboxes. “Too long printing all the cards.” The cause is not the length: those cards are status buttons, Take Photo buttons and a live shot count, all of which are inert on paper. The Job Plan is a control surface, not a document. It will be revisited as a one-page brief — the standing flags, the room list with its statuses, the open phase gates — rather than a fuller dump of the tab. Nothing on screen changed and nothing is lost: the plan, the ticks and the statuses are all still there.
⚠ What went with it: the standing job flags have no paper route now. That panel reached paper only because the print carried the plan header along with the content, so the firearms rule can no longer be handed to somebody on a sheet. It is still the first thing on the Job Plan tab, which is what a phone has open in the house — the surface that actually matters — so read it aloud off the screen. Giving it back is the first thing a revisit has to do.

The plan header shows the documentation level on estate/formal jobs. Chain of custody is mandatory when that level is Formal, the service is Probate or Contested Probate, or the estate is administered through probate (an Estate Settlement included, since 2026-09-29) or, since 2026-10-03, through a trust (a matter recorded Trust or Both, at any level) — it is driven by the documentation level and the matter (§4), not the Premium-estate rate. The §733.604 stream (the court chips and the Florida court & legal compliance list under Job Admin) follows the matter too: an estate administered through probate carries it whatever the service, and one that is not (a trust administration, a family distribution) does not; a trust administration carries the trustee’s own list instead (§10a, since 2026-10-03).

Found on site — an original will, or cash (new 2026-10-03)

Anthony, 2026-10-03, on two things a crew finds in an estate’s house that Havellin must never hold: an original will goes to the estate attorney the same day against a signed receipt and is followed until its deposit with the clerk is confirmed; cash is counted by two people, sealed in a numbered bag and signed for by the fiduciary the same day. On every estate the Job Plan’s In the house stage carries a Found on site card under the in-house boxes, which point down at it, with Record an original will and Record cash found. A living client’s plan has no card, and the dialog and its save both refuse one.

ActWhat is recordedRefused withoutRecorded and flagged
Record an original willthe day it was found, where in the house, who found it, and what it looks like from the outside (it is never opened, read, copied or interpreted); a hand-over beside it if it has already gonethe day, the finder and the description—
Record cash foundthe day it was counted, where, the amount to the cent, both counters and the sealed bag’s number; a hand-over beside it if it has already gonethe day, the amount, two different counters (one person under two spellings is one person) and the bag number—
Record the hand-overto whom (by default the estate attorney recorded, else the representative, for a will; the representative for cash; any name may be typed) and on what day (today by default)the name and the daya day other than the find’s; cash handed to somebody who is not a fiduciary recorded on the job
Confirm the deposit with the clerk (a will)the day it was deposited and who confirmed it (by default whoever holds it)the day and the name, and a hand-over recorded first—

Each record is on the job (job.siteFinds), merged on the sheet entry by entry, and is never edited or deleted: a find’s facts are written once, its hand-over and its deposit are each recorded once, and one recorded in error is voided on the card with the reason and stays listed, struck through. Print Receipt for Original Will and Print Cash Count and Receipt print the form for the hand-over, with blank lines for whatever is not recorded yet. The will’s states that Havellin has not opened it, read its contents or copied it and keeps no copy, and cites the custodian’s duty to deposit it with the clerk within ten days (§732.901); the cash form has a line for each counter and one for the fiduciary, who signs for the bag with its seal intact. Once the hand-over is recorded the find offers File signed receipt: a PDF or a photograph of the signed form, filed to the client’s Signed Records folder and linked on the find. Both forms are drafted, pending counsel.

The reminder. The card’s own lines, and the same lines first on the desk’s Job Admin card (§10a): Original will held by Havellin in red, saying to hand it to the attorney today, until a hand-over is recorded; then Original will handed over against a signed receipt, open until the signed receipt is filed, and Original will deposited with the clerk, open and due ten days after the hand-over, red from the day after, green once the deposit is confirmed. Cash held by Havellin in red until its hand-over; then Cash count receipt filed, open until the count’s signed receipt is filed. A record made on the other device shows after a reload: the plan’s refresh brings rooms and hours, not the client record.

What each service type’s plan actually contains (restructured 2026-09-19)

Asked directly (2026-09-11): “does the job plan change for estate and non-estate jobs? … what do non estate jobs have?” It is one plan, not two. Six of the seven services run the same stage spine and the estate rigor is added to it; only Home Prep for Sale has a different renderer. The 2026-09-19 cut took the checklist from roughly sixty boxes to the handful a person genuinely has to attest to, and replaced the rest with derived lines the app reads off the job record — agreement signed, deposit received, Letters on file, rooms locked, midpoint invoice sent and paid, vendors confirmed, release authority on every item leaving, rooms cleared, hours logged, change orders accepted, final invoice sent, and on an estate the Disposition Ledger signed (since 2026-10-03). A derived line is never ticked; it turns green when the record says so. Counted off the real plan catalogues and gate rules (re-measured for P19 on its merged build: the new chips and lines, and the two financial-close boxes that became lines), same six-room house, no vendors on the estimate, matter and tier unanswered, and on an estate a date of death recorded (every estate records one) with the 706 left at Unknown, its default:

ServiceStages on the threadBoxes on the planDerived linesUnder Job AdminWhat it adds over the one above it
Home Editing4982 boxesThe baseline: the pre-job call, access and crew, one in-house box (every room photographed and every item of consequence logged before it moves, since 2026-09-21), the close-out and the three client asks.
Home Transition52882 boxesNew home prep under Before Day 1 and the whole Move day stage — thirteen boxes, each of them real work a person attests to on the day.
Home Cleanout41383 boxesPickups and disposal under Midpoint & pickups (TC present for every valuables pickup; shredding), home confirmed empty at close-out; the settlement timeline under Job Admin, where the proceeds statements are reconciled as a line the app reads (since 2026-10-03; it was a box).
Estate Settlement416, or 17 on a probate or a trust matter10 (11 with an attorney recorded, 13 on a probate matter, 11 on a trust matter; one fewer with the 706 answered No)3, or 6–9 once the matter is recorded as probate, 5–8 as a trustThe four in-house documentation boxes under the room grid (firearms photographed where they lie; each checked for NFA; documents sequestered against a receipt; cash and jewelry logged) in place of the living-client one, and the written release authority line. On a matter administered through probate it also gets the PR-authority box and the three court chips; on any other matter an estate attorney on file chip appears only when one is recorded (2026-09-29). Since 2026-10-03 every estate also gets the Found on site card, the Disposition Ledger signed line at close-out and, unless the 706 is answered No, the Form 706 chip; a trust matter gets the trust’s twin of the PR-authority box, the Certification of Trust on file chip and the trustee’s desk list.
Probate417138 — 6–9 by tier, 5–8 on a trust matterEverything an Estate Settlement gets on a probate matter, here by default (an unanswered matter on a Probate job counts as probate): the PR-authority box, the Letters of Administration on file, estate attorney on file and §733.604 inventory deadline chips, and the Florida court & legal compliance list under Job Admin. All of them follow the matter type, so a Probate job recorded as a trust administration has none of them and, since 2026-10-03, the trust’s own in their place (17 boxes, 11 lines). Both court chips are answered at intake and corrected on Edit Client: the Letters status under Authorized Representative → Letters of Administration (default Pending; Client Intake has asked it since 2026-09-22, and until then the chip claimed a status no form recorded), and the deadline, which follows the Letters Issued date under Probate Information (on Edit Client since 2026-09-30). A red chip names Edit Client, because mid-job that is the form you can reach.
Contested Probate41713the sameNothing. Identical to Probate, section for section — see the note below.
Home Prep for Sale—9, or 10 with declutter hours authorised—2 boxesA different document entirely (§6c): the coordination checklist, budget & fee, prep-vendor sourcing and, since 2026-09-22, the Close-out card. No stages, no room list, and no hours log unless declutter hours were quoted or an accepted change order added concierge hours (§6c). Since 2026-09-30 an accepted change order can also add a prep vendor, which joins the sourcing list and the budget card (below; §12).

Boxes are the plan’s own ticks plus the Close-out card’s three: the satisfaction call, the referral ask, and Review posted on Google, which appears once the review request is sent. Each collection partner on the estimate adds three more on its sourcing row, and firearms flagged at intake add two on a living-client job (an estate always carries them).

⚠ The Job Admin column is a RANGE now, and that is the point of it (2026-09-21). The desk paperwork under Job Admin no longer follows the service type: the compliance half is decided by the matter type (probate / trust / both / neither) and the engagement tier (§10a), so the same Probate job renders nine boxes at Inventory + appraisals, six at None and five to eight if the matter is recorded as a trust administration (the trustee’s own list, since 2026-10-03). Until 2026-10-03 those figures were twelve, nine and a flat five: the PR’s sign-off box went, two financial-close boxes became lines the app reads, and a trust matter gained the trustee’s list. The figures above are the whole measured range; the plan boxes and the derived lines beside them do not move with the tier. The appraisal box among them (All professional appraisals attached as supporting documentation) is there where the tier puts every appraisal with Havellin or an appraiser is on the job’s roster and, since 2026-09-30 (Q20), wherever the approved estimate lists an appraiser, at any tier: a None job with an appraiser line renders seven.
Two boxes are gone for good, and the lines that replace them cannot be ticked. Midpoint invoice sent and Midpoint payment received were checkboxes on the plan that recorded nothing — the invoice record and the payments list already knew both. They are read from those now (docSentAt and stagePaidTotal), the invoice stage on the Client Dashboard is chosen off the same record, and the two booleans are deleted from the job. Letters on file likewise reads intake’s executor-authorization field. A box a person could tick without the thing having happened was the defect; the app now says what it knows and asks only for what it cannot know.
⚠ Ticks made before 2026-09-19 do not carry. The old boxes were keyed by their position in a list, so a tick was the fourth box in Phase 0; the new ones are named (PLAN_TASKS, keys like crew_briefed), so a tick survives the list being reordered or a box being added above it — and an old positional tick has nothing to attach to. Room statuses, plan notes, hours, photographs and the whole Job Admin & Inv tab carried. Prelaunch, dummy data only.
⚠ Contested Probate and Probate produce an identical plan, and that is worth knowing before somebody goes looking for the difference. Everything contested-specific lives elsewhere: the heavier pricing coefficients on Build Estimate, the wider 35% fixed-price contingency (§5c — until 2026-09-20 a flat fee was withheld from both probate services outright), documentation always at Formal, and the $500 appraisal threshold (§4; a dispute recorded on any other estate does the same for both). The executor-authorisation blocker is not one of them: it stops any estate administered through probate from activating. The plan treats the two the same because the work in the house is the same — a dispute changes who may authorise a release, not how a room is sorted. The per-item Disputed flag is what carries it onto the release approval request (§10a).
A room is drawn ONCE now (2026-09-19), and the two-grid notes this section carried are history. Until this date every room rendered twice — a Phase 1 grid that capped it at locked and a Phase 2 grid that owned packed → complete — so that the crew could not drive a room to complete from Phase 1 and skip the midpoint invoice with it. The midpoint is read off the invoice record now, so the guard the second grid existed for is enforced where the money is, and the room list (§10) carries the two taps instead: Lock, then Cleared, in the room’s own workspace, with Cleared refused until the room is locked. A room at pending no longer has a row of grey buttons anywhere to read as state that failed to arrive. The one real exception recorded on 2026-09-12 still stands: anything locked on the other device before the Apps Script redeploy of 2026-09-11 is genuinely gone; re-lock it once.
⚠⚠ THE JOB PLAN NOW FOLLOWS THE OTHER DEVICE WHILE YOU HAVE IT OPEN (new 2026-09-12). Until this date the plan and the hours log were read once, when the page loaded, and never again. So the 2026-09-11 fix that stopped two people overwriting each other’s plan was correct in the sheet and invisible on screen: the house locked a room and the desk went on showing it pending until somebody reloaded. The tab now re-reads roughly every 20 seconds while it is open, and again the moment you enter it, so a room locked in the house appears at the desk on its own. ⚠ It does not bring vendors or the team — those are saved on the client, not on the plan, so the other device’s changes to them show only after a reload. Until the Apps Script 2026-09-30 redeploy, a change you make to them on a copy loaded that morning takes the whole client (§2, 2026-09-29); from it, each vendor line and each team slot merges on its own (§2). Reload before changing either on a client somebody else has been working. Nothing else in the app polls — the client list still refreshes on page load (and while an estimate is awaiting approval), so if you want the Client Dashboard current, reload.

⚠ Two things it deliberately will not do. It never refreshes while you are typing in the plan — a redraw replaces the whole tab and would take a half-written note with it — so it waits until you click away. And it stands down completely while any of your own changes are still waiting to be saved (a write still on its way, or the red chip bottom-left for one that is retrying): until your write has landed, the sheet’s copy is older than yours, and pulling it in would undo a room you had just locked. Since 2026-09-30 it also throws away an answer that left before you changed something, so a room, note or hour you changed while it was fetching is never put back. Each case simply waits for the next pass.

Staffing the job — do this before any hours exist

Staffing is an explicit step, and it gates hours logging. The window for it is the wait between sending the agreement and the deposit landing: you know the job is happening, and you now find out who is actually free.

  1. Open Hours & daily close on the Job Plan (it never opens by itself); the Job Team & Hours roster is in it. It renders the crew the approved estimate was priced for — not a flat six slots — so you can't quietly log hours for specialists nobody quoted. A concierge row is always there, and a Transition Concierge 2 row when the estimate staffs or suggests a second concierge, or one is already named.
  2. Confirm each person's availability, then name them. Any planned slot still empty is flagged "needs a name".
  3. Need a body beyond what was quoted? + Add a specialist beyond plan. Those rows carry a beyond plan tag, because crew above plan raises cost against a fixed quote and the projection will flag it.
  4. Hit Save & Confirm Job Team →.
Two separate gates, and both must hold before hours can be logged. Confirming the team is the first, and it is meant to be done before the client pays. The second is the deposit: no hours against a job that hasn't paid its 50% deposit (a payment within 1% of it counts), and nothing overrides it. A confirmed team on an unfunded job keeps its confirmation and says plainly that the deposit is what's missing — staffing ahead of payment is deliberate, starting work is not. If the deposit is part-paid the bar names what has arrived and what is outstanding.

Only once both hold does Save Hours Entry become available. Until the team is signed off the hours boxes are inert on purpose — the roster is a staffing sheet at that point, not a timesheet, and doing both in one confused pass is how the wrong person ends up with the hours.

  • Placeholders. Contractor TBD (specialist) and Contractor — TC (an outside concierge) stand for a confirmed need with an unconfirmed person. Allowed — you often have the headcount before the names — but they cost at a placeholder rate, so the app says out loud that margin on the job is an estimate until they're named.
  • Confirming locks the named members. Empty slots stay open, so someone can still be added mid-job.
  • Revise team reopens it — but anyone who has already logged hours stays locked. You can add to a team mid-job; you can never retroactively remove someone who worked. Hours can't be logged again until the team is re-confirmed.
  • One person, one slot — refused the moment it is picked (2026-09-23). A name taken on the roster disappears from the other selects, and picking a name another slot already holds is refused on the spot, naming the slot that has them; Save & Confirm Job Team refuses a duplicate too, as a backstop. Until this date the rule lived only in which names each select offered, and a select’s options are drawn once — so two quick picks could put Anthony Jr in both specialist slots, and the team confirmed that way. Only the placeholders above may legitimately repeat. A team confirmed with a duplicate before today shows a red Job team confirmed chip: press Revise team and give one of the slots to somebody else.
  • A second concierge the estimate staffs needs a name too (2026-09-29). When the estimate’s own box says the scope needs a second concierge (§5c), Save & Confirm Job Team refuses while the Transition Concierge 2 row is empty, and the row reads needs a name: pick a concierge, or Contractor — TC for one not picked yet.
