The specialists already had this rule and the concierges did not, so a job priced with two concierges — and on a rush job promised two on Exhibit A — confirmed with the second slot empty and nothing said who was coming. A row the engine merely suggests, with the estimate’s box unticked, stays optional.
  • The concierge row starts on the concierge assigned at intake (2026-09-23), not on whoever did the walkthrough. The two usually agree; on a job Ashley was assigned at intake and Anthony walked, the plan named Anthony. Until somebody picks a concierge on the Job Plan or the team has ever been confirmed, the row follows Edit Client — reassign the job there and the plan follows. Once a concierge is picked or the team has been confirmed, that choice stands.

Log Hours (labor jobs)

One entry per working day: date, an activity summary, and hours against each crew member on the confirmed roster. Roles come from the roster, not a dropdown — the concierge rows log as TC (including the second concierge), the specialist rows as PS. Hours accumulate against the estimate and drive the forward-variance projection. Log daily — do not batch at end of job.

⚠⚠ Hours are logged in half hours (since 2026-10-02, P18). Anthony: “I think actually billing every 15mins is a lot of detail…we are not lawyers billing $1500/hr. maybe make it thirty minutes for logging hours (so a TC can bill 1hr, 1.5hrs or 2hrs)”. Every hours box on the form steps by 0.5, and Save Hours Entry refuses any row that is not a whole number of half hours — both concierge rows and every crew row — naming each row it refuses and saving nothing, the good rows beside it included: “Hours are logged in half hours (0.5, 1.0, 1.5 …). Not a half hour: Anthony Graziano Jr (1.25). Correct it and save again; nothing was saved.” 1.5 is logged as 1.5. The final invoice bills exactly the hours logged at the rate, to the cent: 1.5 hours at $185 is $277.50, and nothing is rounded up. An entry logged before today in quarter hours (P17’s one day, 2026-10-01) or in tenths stays as it was — a 2.25 shows as 2.25 and bills 2.25 × the rate — and the hours readouts sum the log to the hundredth. The coordination hours recorded against a vendor on the sourcing list (below) take half hours too. A change order’s hours are whole hours (§9), and an estimate’s, the Pre-Prep Declutter box’s included, are rounded up to the whole hour (§5i, §6a-i); for P17’s day every one of these took quarter hours. Both agreements say so: “Time is recorded and billed in half-hour increments, as worked.” (§8).
⚠ On a fixed-price job, more than 15% over the hours is a margin warning, never a stop (2026-09-25). The hours summary, the projection card, the line under the hours fold and the Hours Log on the Client Dashboard all read the time-and-materials rule on every job — “Client must be notified in writing before continuing work” and “⛔ STOP — Change Order Required”. On a flat fee neither is true: the fee does not move with the hours, so an overrun on the agreed scope is ours to absorb and comes out of the margin, and neither agreement’s fixed arm carries an hours trigger. They read “⚠ Over the estimated hours — margin at risk” now, amber and red at the same lines as before, and say the one thing still worth doing: if the extra time is work beyond the agreed scope, raise a change order before doing more of it (§9). The time-and-materials rule is unchanged; its two wordings, which disagreed about whether the notice had to be in writing, are one sentence now: “The client must be notified in writing before work continues — per the agreement.”
⚠ The hours summary measures against the hours the client has authorised (2026-09-25): the estimate plus every accepted change order. A job holding a signed +40-hour change order no longer reads red, or tells anybody to stop, over those forty hours; the line under the table says Estimated hours include … from 1 accepted change order, so the bigger figure is never mistaken for the estimate’s own. Hours past the authorised figure still raise the same alerts, and an unaccepted change order moves nothing (§9).

Vendor & Partner Sourcing

Assign a directory vendor to each estimate line and logged category, set status, and record the actual quote. Actual quotes on home-prep lines feed the 30% Home Sale Preparation Fee on the midpoint and final invoices — on a standalone prep job and, since 2026-09-10, on prep bundled into a labor job too. Every other vendor line carries no Havellin fee at all; those are recorded so the client's pass-through costs and the job's true margin are known. The Coord hrs box does not appear on a prep line, because prep books no coordination hours to check a recorded figure against. On standalone Home Prep the list includes any vendor an accepted change order added (since 2026-09-30, §9): it follows the estimate’s lines, with its estimated cost as the budget, and is booked and quoted like them. It counts on the budget card, in the fold’s quoted count, in Prep vendors confirmed on the Job Admin desk card and, once Confirmed, on the close-out scorecard; its Quote $ is what the 30% fee is taken on (§12). A change order the client has not accepted adds nothing here.

Coord hrs — optional, and it bills nothing. Beside each vendor's actual quote is a Coord hrs box with the estimate's figure next to it (est 3.0). Roughly how long that vendor actually took to coordinate. It exists because the touch counts in §5d-i are currently judgement and nothing in the app could ever check them — the hours log records a date, an activity note and who worked, with no way to attribute an hour to this mover. A few real jobs of these and the counts tune themselves off evidence instead of intuition. Fill it in when it's easy and skip it when it isn't; nothing depends on it and no gate consults it.

It is not billed, and cannot be — this is the part worth understanding, because it looks like it should be. The client is billed off your logged hours, which already contain the time you spent phoning that mover; it simply isn't labelled. So this is a breakdown of hours already logged and already invoiced, not extra hours. Feeding it into pricing would bill the same time twice. Once anything is recorded, a line at the foot of the section totals it: estimated 8.5 hrs · recorded 5.5 hrs across 1 vendor. Not shown on a standalone Home Prep job, which books no vendor-coordination hours — the 30% fee covers that — and therefore has no estimate to check against.
⚠ End-of-job logistics are the vendors the job needs, and nothing else (2026-09-23). Until this date the Job Plan carried five standing logistics rows on every labour job — donation organization, junk removal / hauling, dumpster rental, move-out / final cleaning, document shredding — so a job that never needed a shredder showed a Document Shredding line waiting for a vendor. Anthony: “it should just be the vendors that you choose when you create the estimate.” The list is the estimate’s vendors now, sourced as estimate lines. When a job turns out mid-way to need one of the five, pick it from + Add an end-of-job vendor… under the list: it becomes a line to source like any other, counts toward vendors lined up, and carries a Remove button. Removing one that has a vendor or a quote on it asks first, naming what comes off the job — the vendor, and its quote, which comes off the vendor costs the invoices list (since 2026-09-30; it used to say the quote was no longer billed, though Havellin never bills a vendor’s quote: the client pays the vendor directly). A category the estimate already carries is never offered, so nothing is asked twice, and a line sourced before today with a vendor, a quote or a status on it stays on the job.

Every line with a vendor assigned names who to call, and each number beside whom it reaches (since 2026-09-30): the firm, its office line labelled office, then each named contact from the directory beside their own mobile (§13) — Acme Hauling · ☎ (561) 555-0100 office · Andy Ramirez ☎ (561) 555-0199 mobile. Every number dials. It used to print contact 1’s name and then the firm’s office number, which read as that person’s direct line and rang the switchboard — the defect the Vendors card had until its contacts were split.

An end-of-job vendor you didn't know you needed (donation, junk removal, a dumpster, final cleaning or shredding) is added the same way: pick it from + Add an end-of-job vendor…, assign the vendor, log the quote. The vendor invoices the client directly at cost, and a Havellin invoice carries no vendor line and no fee on it. No other kind of vendor can be added on a labour job's plan. If the job also changes Havellin's scope or hours, that is a Change Order (§9) — the vendor line alone is not one. On Home Prep for Sale a new prep vendor comes in through a change order (since 2026-09-30, §9): once the client accepts it, the vendor joins the prep sourcing list to be booked and quoted, and the 30% Home Sale Preparation Fee applies to what it actually charges (§12).

12. Invoices

Client Dashboard → open the job → the Deposit / Midpoint / Final invoice sent rows on the timeline (§9a). Each row is its own invoice — there is no stage picker any more, because the row you are on is the stage. The Invoices tab was retired from the navigation on 2026-09-11. The three stages are unchanged: Deposit is 50% of the approved estimate. Midpoint (the Second invoice on Home Prep) asks for the cumulative 75% less what the deposit actually brought in, with the home prep fee trued to the actual quotes logged on the Job Plan; labour stays on the estimate until the final. Final is the whole total less everything received: on time and materials, labour is trued to the logged hours (which already include any accepted change order’s hours); on a fixed price each accepted change order is added at the price printed on it (§9); either way the prep fee is trued to actuals. Third-party vendors bill the client directly and never appear on a Havellin invoice.

The Service row names the service as every client document does (2026-10-05, P20). On a Probate or Contested Probate service whose matter is recorded Trust or Neither it reads Estate Settlement or Contested Estate Settlement, and so does the line under the invoice email’s heading (§4). The amounts are the service’s own.
⚠⚠ Money is carried to the cent (since 2026-10-01, P17). Every figure the app derives — the fees, the premium and the discount, the prep fee, the 50 / 25 / 25 split, each invoice’s balance, a change order’s price — is taken to the cent (half a cent rounds up, away from zero) by one helper, and printed by one formatter that shows the cents whenever there are any: $971.25, $1,234.50, and $900 still $900. A $21,679.50 estimate (a Premium Estate Settlement on a 3,500 sqft house with a 3% preferred-client discount, re-measured 2026-10-02 on P18’s whole hours; this example was a $22,071.25 estimate on P17’s quarter hours) asks a $10,839.75 deposit, a $5,419.88 midpoint and a $5,419.87 final, and the three add up to it; until P17 every figure was rounded to the whole dollar. The rows of every client document add up to its own totals to the cent, and the ACH payment link asks for exactly the invoice’s outstanding figure (§8b): $5,839.75 after a $5,000 cheque on that deposit, where before P17 it asked the whole dollar, $5,840. A price a client was sent or accepted is recorded to the cent too, so a raise of a cent asks again (§9a). The money boxes keep cents as typed ($1,234.50; one typed “1234.50” used to become $123,450) and offer a phone the keypad with a decimal point.
Most invoices need no PIN. Deposit and midpoint are formulaic from the approved estimate — nothing to review, so they print and send straight out. Only the final can need a manager PIN, and only when it lands more than ±15% from the estimate. Opening, printing or sending it is then refused with This final is N% off the estimate, outside the ±15% tolerance, so it needs a manager PIN before it can be issued, and 🔑 Manager approval beside the lit Final invoice sent step takes the PIN; pressed on a final inside the tolerance, it says no approval is needed, and on an hourly final with no hours logged it says what blocks it (the blocked final, below). An accepted change order moves the estimate side of that comparison by what the final bills for it, since the client agreed to it separately: its hours at the job’s rates (on a fixed price, its signed price), and the 30% fee on a prep vendor it adds, at the estimated cost (below). Since 2026-09-30 (P16) that includes the expedited premium and the preferred-client discount the final bills on an hourly change order’s hours. The baseline moved by those hours at the plain rates, so on an expedited job with a 10% discount a change order for $10,000 of hours read +$800 of variance on a final that billed exactly the authorised hours; it reads none now. One rule (coBaselineMove) moves the invoice’s baseline and the client list’s Havellin Est. alike. Only the final is ever approved, and the approval belongs to that client and that balance (below). (The No approval required badge and the banner naming the direction, over or under, belonged to the retired Invoices tab; the refusal names the percentage only.)
⚠⚠ A manager’s PIN approves ONE client’s final at ONE figure, and it is kept with that client (fixed 2026-09-29). Until this date the approval lived in the open browser page rather than on the job. Measured with two clients each 30% over their estimate: the PIN typed for one client from its dashboard released the other client’s final as well, an approved final went on printing after more hours were logged and its balance moved, and a reload forgot the approval entirely. The approval is now recorded on the client it was typed for — who, when, and the balance they were shown — and syncs with that client to the other device.

It counts only while the final still asks for that figure. More hours logged, an accepted change order, or a payment recorded after the PIN all move the balance, and then the final asks again: the refusal names who approved it, at what figure, and what it asks for now. Put the figure back — a mis-typed hours line corrected — and the approval on file counts again. Pressing 🔑 Manager approval on a final already approved at today’s figure does not ask twice; it says who approved it and when.

The Approved for Release band now shows on the approved final when you view it — the app built it and had never drawn it. It is ours, not the client’s: it is hidden when the invoice is printed, and hidden in the PDF that is emailed or filed.
⚠ THE FINAL CARRIES NO “job ran over estimate” LINE — removed 2026-09-11, and the absence is the decision. It used to print “Job ran over estimate by $1,360 — client was notified per T&Cs” on every positive variance however small — a claim the app has never had a record of at any size, since that conversation happens mid-job and what the system can evidence is an accepted change order. Anthony’s call on the whole row: “i don’t think there is any need to flag a minor over run. and an over run of 15% should not happen b/c at 15% we require a change order, and if that is approved, the job will not be ‘over run’ b/c the additional amount is flagged and agreed to. an estimate is an estimate, not a guarantee of the final number.”

The second half is the structural argument. An accepted change order moves the baseline the variance is measured against (§9), so on a job run the way the agreement describes, authorised scope is inside the estimate by construction and a real overrun cannot reach the final at all. One that does means the change order was skipped — which is a thing to fix in the office, not a sentence to print at a client who was never asked to agree to it.

Both directions went, not just the overage. The Payment Summary already prints Original Estimate and Actual Havellin services total on rows inches apart, so the line was the subtraction of two numbers already on the page; and a document that narrates the favourable direction while going quiet on the other reads as selective. Nothing is hidden and nothing about what is billed changed.
⚠ The ±15% manager PIN is internal and is deliberately untouched by that removal. A final more than ±15% off the estimate still stops for a manager. That is the control that catches the skipped change order above — withholding the document until somebody looks is the right response to that state, and telling the client about it on the invoice is not. Do not “finish the job” by removing the gate as well.
⚠ A $1 CREDIT TO A CLIENT WHO PAID CORRECTLY — fixed 2026-09-11, and the balance was never wrong. The summary’s “Received ahead of the invoiced schedule — credited in the balance below” line compared what the earlier invoices asked for against what arrived — with both figures already rounded to the dollar, so the guard meant to swallow bank rounding (below a dollar, say nothing) could never observe anything smaller than one. On a $25,715 estate the stage targets land on part dollars (50% is $12,857.50, 75% is $19,286.25); the client paid the exact quarter, $6,428.75, we had asked for the rounded $6,428, and a fifty-cent difference printed as a ($1) credit. Both gap comparisons now measure the real figures and round once for display. The balance due is untouched — it was always correct, because the basis is whole dollars. Only the notice was wrong.
⚠ FIVE THINGS ON THE INVOICES NOW MATCH WHAT WAS SIGNED (2026-09-29).

The final’s Original Estimate row is the estimate. It printed the estimate plus the accepted change orders’ hours at the billing rates, so a $10,962 job with a +10/+10 change order showed $13,462 above a $5,481 deposit and a $2,741 midpoint that were plainly half and a quarter of something else. It prints the estimate alone now — the basis the advance payments were taken on — with Approved Change Orders on its own line directly under it.

The midpoint and final bill the signed schedule to the dollar. They re-rounded the cumulative 75% on their own, so on a total that does not split evenly the midpoint came out a dollar off the payment schedule the client signed and the final a dollar off the other way — 12 of 79 jobs measured. They now bill the schedule’s own running targets, the same arithmetic the estimate and the agreement print — to the cent since 2026-10-01 (P17), the schedule itself being split to the cent (§12).

A credit reads as a credit in the email. A final that comes in under what the deposit and midpoint already collected is correct, and the invoice has always printed it as a green Credit; the covering email said “Balance due: $-2,741 … Payment is due within 7 calendar days.” It reads “Credit to you: $2,741 — Nothing is due on this invoice. We will be in touch to arrange the credit.”

On a Premium Estate final the people add up to their role. Two people sharing a role at a rate that lands on half a dollar each rounded up ($185 × 10.3 = $1,905.50, printed twice as $1,906 over a $3,811 total); the remainder rode on one row from then, and since 2026-10-01 (P17) each row prints its exact share, $1,905.50 and $1,905.50, money being carried to the cent.

The refusal names the invoice. An invoice drawn off an unapproved estimate refused with “…before the agreement can be drawn”; it says invoice now.
The final invoice is blocked outright until hours are logged. On a time-and-materials labor job with no hours recorded there is no PIN, no PDF and no email — a manager cannot unlock it, because nobody can approve a total that has nothing behind it. The final bills labor at the hours actually worked, so the Job Plan timesheet is the invoice. This catches a dry run especially: skip the daily logging on a practice job and the final simply will not issue. Log the hours, then reprint. (Fixed-price and fee-only jobs are exempt — their labor total does not come from the log.) 🔑 Manager approval on such a final says what the send refuses with, “No hours have been logged for this job, so the final cannot be issued. Log them on the Job Plan tab first.” (since 2026-09-30, P16; it said the final was inside the ±15% tolerance and needed no approval, and the send then refused it).
⚠ A Home Prep job whose hours came only from a change order is warned, not blocked (2026-09-25). The block above is for a job whose estimate priced hours — that includes a prep job that quoted declutter hours. On a vendors-only prep job an accepted change order can add concierge hours (§9); the final bills the logged ones at the change order’s rate on top of the 30% fee, with a line under the change orders saying they are the concierge (TC) hours in the table. If none are logged the final still issues, billing the fee alone — an accepted change order cannot be withdrawn, so a refusal there could never be lifted if the work turned out not to be needed. The timeline’s Final invoice sent step and the desk card both say so before it goes, and the ±15% manager PIN still stops a final that lands more than 15% short of the estimate and the change orders together. Log the hours.
⚠ A Home Prep change order can add a prep vendor, and the invoices bill it like the others (2026-09-30). Anthony: “yes, add new also, or add TC hours for decluttering.” On standalone Home Prep for Sale a change order can add a preparation vendor found mid-job, with or without concierge hours (§9); since P16, the same day, so can a labour job whose estimate bundles prep, and its invoices bill the vendor the same way inside their prep fee row, hourly or fixed. Once the client accepts it, the vendor is one of the job’s prep lines (§11): it bills the client directly at cost, and the 30% Home Sale Preparation Fee is taken on it from the Second invoice on — on its estimated cost, tagged est., until its Quote $ is typed on the Job Plan, then on the quote. The final’s change-order row names it, Adds Painting (est. $4,500) — it bills you directly, and the Home Sale Preparation Fee on it is in the fee above, and carries no price of its own; the payment summary’s change-order line reads adds Painting, in the Home Sale Preparation Fee above. Measured on a $45,000 prep job: an accepted $4,500 painter adds $1,350 to what the job collects, and $1,500 once it quotes $5,000. An unaccepted change order adds nothing. The ±15% comparison moves with it, like an accepted change order’s hours: its fee at the estimated cost the client accepted counts as authorised, so an accepted vendor alone never sends the final to a manager, and only what it actually charges beyond that estimate counts against the tolerance. (Built 2026-09-30 after this pass measured the opposite: a $9,000 painter on the same job read +20% and asked for the PIN.)
A Home Prep final says what its total is made of (2026-09-30). The Payment Summary heads the total Services total (Home Sale Preparation Fee on actual vendor spend + logged concierge hours), or Services total (Home Sale Preparation Fee on actual vendor spend) when no concierge hours are logged — Anthony’s wording. It read Actual Havellin services total (logged hours + actual fees), which every other hourly final still carries, on a job that often logs no hours at all. The figure under it is unchanged.

The app also works out a softer warning — crew hours logged with no concierge hours against them, naming the dollar value of concierge time the final is probably missing. From the retirement of the Invoices tab (2026-09-11) no screen showed it; since 2026-09-30 (P16) the timeline’s Final invoice sent row carries it (§9a), from the close until the final goes out, and the band says it while that row is lit: “60.0 crew hours logged with no concierge hours. The concierge is on site for every crew hour, so this final is likely under-billing $12,000 of concierge time. Check the log before sending.” The figure is the concierge time the estimate priced, at the job’s concierge rate, and it is never said on a fixed price or a fee-only job, whose totals do not come from the log (finalCrewOnlyWarn, the one rule the invoice reads too). Check the hours log before sending an hourly final: specialist hours with no concierge hours beside them nearly always mean the log is incomplete, because the concierge is on site for every crew hour. The final issues either way.

That describes a time-and-materials job, which is the default. On a job quoted fixed price (§5c) the flat fee is the base of the Havellin services total — the stages split that total and labor is never trued to logged hours — and these sit on their own lines around it:

  • the 30% home prep fee, where the job carries prep vendors, trued to the actual quotes from the midpoint exactly as on time and materials (until 2026-09-23 it was folded inside the flat fee and never trued, while this paragraph already said it was);
  • on an estimate saved from 2026-09-30, the expedited-delivery premium (20% of the fee) and the preferred-client discount (§5c), on all three stages; an older fixed fee carries both inside it and prints neither;
  • each accepted change order at the price printed on it (§9), in its own Amount column in the Approved Change Orders table. On an expedited fixed price saved from 2026-09-30, a change order raised from then on carries the 20% premium in that price ($2,000 of hours prints as $2,400; the Q14 follow-up, Anthony: “yes”), and the final adds exactly the printed figure; one raised before, or on an older fixed fee, keeps its plain-rate price. The preferred-client discount never applies to a change order.

So a fixed-price final issues with nothing in the hours log and needs no PIN unless the prep vendors’ actual invoices move it more than 15% (measured 2026-09-20 on a $23,000 fee: $11,500 · $5,750 · $5,750, zero hours). The log stays open on the job regardless (§9a-i), and it is kept on every job, fixed price included — Anthony’s decision of 2026-09-20, mandatory for the transition concierge: “we are gonna learn from the hours and the discipline of setting a job timeline and sticking to it and not letting jobs run over that are fixed cost and therefore destroying our own margins.” The hours are the only record of whether the fee’s contingency (20%; 25% on Probate, 35% on Contested Probate) was right, and of a fixed-price job running past the days it was priced on — which is the one thing that turns a flat fee into a loss. So the fold’s amber no hours logged today is deliberately not quieted on a fixed-price job, and nothing on the hours path branches on the billing basis (measured: the same form, the same two gates — the team sign-off and the deposit — and the same fold and strip readings on a flat fee as on time and materials).

Sending an invoice files it to Drive as its draft is made, into the job's Invoice folder (the job folder itself if that subfolder is missing), one file per stage — HVL-xxxx - Havellin Invoice - Deposit.html, … - Midpoint.html (… - Second.html on Home Prep) and … - Final.html — so the three never overwrite each other. Re-filing a stage replaces its own copy, which is what you want when a figure is corrected and reissued. Printing does not file, so an invoice only printed and handed over is kept nowhere: send it. If that filing does not land, 📁 File deposit invoice to Drive (and so on) is offered in the band’s tray and the strip once the send is confirmed; once filed they read 📁 Filed deposit invoice, which opens the copy (§9b). (Changed 2026-09-11. Until then only Print on the retired Invoices tab filed an invoice, as HVL-xxxx_Invoice_Deposit.html and so on, and an invoice sent from the timeline was kept nowhere.)

You no longer pick a stage — the timeline lights the invoice that is due. Each of the three has its own row, and the light sits on the earliest step not yet done: Deposit invoice sent, then Deposit received, then Job active. From activation the band reads Do the work — the midpoint invoice is due at the halfway point, with the send as an outline button reading ✉ Send midpoint invoice — due around that day, until the calendar halfway, half the work done or every room locked, whichever comes first (§9a, 2026-09-29); then Midpoint invoice sent, Midpoint payment, Work complete (■ Close job) and the final. Since 2026-09-29 closing the job moves the light straight to the final, whatever the midpoint says (§9a), and Re-open moves it back (§9). The three are separate records, so a midpoint can never inherit a final’s gate.
Historical: when there was a single Stage selector it read a flag that nothing ever set, so every funded job stuck on the Midpoint stage and the Final invoice could only be reached by picking it by hand.

⚠ The final names only the invoices that actually went out (2026-09-29). Since a job can be closed with the midpoint unpaid (§9a), the final can be the first bill after the deposit. It used to print 25% midpoint — invoiced at project midpoint and Outstanding from the deposit and midpoint invoices whether or not a midpoint invoice had ever existed. The money was always right; only the words were off. Now:
  • A midpoint invoice counts as issued when the timeline has it sent, or when a midpoint payment is recorded — a client who paid it was plainly asked for it, and a printed copy handed over leaves no send record. That is the same test the timeline’s midpoint row reads, so the invoice and the timeline cannot tell two stories.
  • When one was issued the final reads exactly as before.
  • When none was, the midpoint row reads 25% midpoint — billed on this invoice at its own 25% share, and a gap names the deposit invoice alone: Outstanding from the deposit invoice — carried into the balance below, or Received ahead of the invoiced schedule when the deposit came in over.
  • The midpoint invoice’s own gap row names itself too. A short deposit reads Outstanding from the deposit invoice — carried into the payment due below; it used to say the deposit and midpoint invoices on the very page that is the midpoint invoice.
  • The no-hours block (above) says the figure was already invoiced at the deposit, and quotes the deposit, when no midpoint invoice went out.
  • The balance due does not move on any job: it is the total less what was actually received, never less what was invoiced. A final printed before today on a job whose midpoint never went out carries the old wording over the right figure; reprint it if the wording matters to the client.
The expedited-delivery premium now appears on the midpoint and final invoices. On a rush job (§5, §16) the 20% premium shows as its own line on all three stages (on a fixed price saved before 2026-09-30 it sits inside the fee and has no line). It previously appeared on the quote, the client estimate and the deposit invoice, but was dropped from the midpoint and final — so a rush client was asked for a deposit that included the premium and then billed a total that didn't, and a job that landed exactly on its estimate printed a phantom "came in under estimate — credit applied" line for the premium amount. If you have invoiced a rush job before now, re-print its midpoint and final stages and check the totals against what the client agreed.

The premium's rate is pinned to the estimate the client accepted, so changing the standard 20% later never reprices a live job. On time and materials the amount trues up with each stage, so the final invoice charges the premium on the services actually delivered — never on the 30% home prep fee, on an estimate saved from 2026-09-30 (Q9). On time and materials a change order adds no separate charge at all — its hours are billed through the timesheet like every other hour, so the premium falls on them exactly as it falls on the rest. Driven on the real invoice: a rush job whose estimate carried 80 TC + 60 PS hours, plus a +20/+20 change order all logged, prints an expedited line of $4,600 against the $3,600 the estimate quoted. On a fixed price the premium line is 20% of the fee alone, and a change order is added at the price printed on it, never through that line: on an expedited fixed price saved from 2026-09-30, a change order raised from then on already carries the 20% in its price (above), so the final adds exactly the printed figure; one raised before, or on an older fixed fee whose premium sits inside it, keeps its plain-rate price. (Corrected 2026-09-11 — this said the premium is never charged on a change order, which was true while a change order carried a dollar amount and is not true now that it carries hours. Corrected again 2026-09-30 — it said the premium was inside the flat fee anyway, which a fixed price saved from that day no longer is.)

12a. Contractors (Crew Directory)

Tab: Contractors. The people who actually work the jobs — concierges and property specialists. It feeds the concierge pickers (intake's concierge and site visit, Edit Client, Build Estimate's Estimate prepared by) and every Job Plan roster, where the specialists are named. Unlike Vendors and Referral Partners, contractors live in the main job sheet.

  • Role — TC (Transition Concierge) or PS (Property Specialist).
  • Rate ($/hr — your cost) — what we pay this person. It is internal, never billed, and it is what makes the margin panel real: a named person is costed at their own rate rather than a placeholder. Keep it current — a blank or stale rate is the single most common reason a job's margin reads wrong. See Section 16.
  • Status — Active · Vetting · Inactive. Only Active crew appear in the staffing dropdowns (the built-in team always does); Toggle on a card flips Active and Inactive, and takes a Vetting contractor to Active.
  • Client-facing bio — concierges only. Two or three sentences; it appears on the client estimate and every invoice, so write it for a client, not for us.
  • Specialty / notes — internal ("strong on furniture, great with families, available weekends"). This is what you read before staffing a difficult estate.
  • Search the crew by name, phone, email, or specialty, and tap to call / text / email straight from the card — this is the field-use path when you need a body tomorrow morning.
  • Order — both columns read alphabetically by the name on the card (since 2026-09-20). The built-in team (Anthony, Ashley and Anthony Jr) sit in their alphabetical place wearing the Team badge rather than pinned to the top, and the staffing dropdowns read in the same order.
  • Quick edit changes phone, email, cost rate, status and notes; Full edit → opens the whole record, name, role and bio included.
  • ✕ Delete (manager PIN) — the ✕ on a card asks for the manager PIN in the same dialog the vendor and partner deletes use (since 2026-09-30; it was a bare Remove this contractor?). A contractor named on any job is not deleted — as the concierge, the site visit, the estimate’s preparer, a crew slot or an hours entry, lost and closed jobs included: the ✕ refuses before the PIN and names the jobs. Retire them instead: set their status to Inactive (Full edit → Status). They leave every staffing dropdown and stay on every job that names them, and the documents that look them up by name keep their contact line. The check runs again after the PIN, and it refuses while the client list has not loaded. The built-in team has no ✕.
A contractor's own rate always wins over the Settings placeholder rates. The four Settings values exist for the unnamed slots you staff before you know who's coming — once a real name is on the roster, that person's record is the cost.

13. Vendor Directory

Tab: Vendors. The curated network of trades and service providers (painters, landscapers, movers, appraisers, haulers, etc.), stored in its own Google Sheet. Work each vendor through its lifecycle: Identified → Contacted → Vetting → Active (with Backup and Do Not Use as terminal states). Only Active vendors can be assigned to a job in the Job Plan sourcing. Status is set with the color-coded dropdown on the card (under the vendor's category), which also shows the star rating beside it, or, until the vendor has been rated on a job, our read — there is no separate status badge.

  • ☎ Log contact — type what you discussed in the note field, then log it; this stamps today as the last-contacted date and stores the latest contact note (used for follow-up cadence). Logging the first contact auto-advances an Identified vendor to Contacted (it never moves a vendor already at Vetting/Active/etc.). The last-contacted date + note show in a highlighted bar right under the log box, so you see the prior touch before you call. Last-contacted is set only this way — it is read-only in the edit form. The old bulk-imported Google review is no longer shown or editable; what surfaces is the star rating earned from completed jobs.
  • Our read — Eager, Neutral or Difficult: how they are to deal with. Set it on the card, directly under Log contact, on the first call, and keep it current. Within a category vendors sort best first on stars (65%) and our read (35%); the filters above the list narrow by category, status, read and rating, and a tile counts the Eager ones.
  • Search — free text across name, category, service area, notes and every contact field: each contact's first and last name, their title, their own email address, and the digits of the office line and of both contact mobiles. Multiple words are AND-ed (so "west palm mover" narrows rather than widens), and a numeric query matches a number however it's punctuated, so typing a mobile you've been called from finds the firm it belongs to.
  • Tap to call / text / email straight from the vendor card, one row per party and each row says who it reaches — ☎ Office and @ Office email for the firm, then ☎ Andy / ✉ Text Andy for each contact who has a mobile on file. Text is only ever offered on a mobile: a switchboard does not receive SMS. In the Job Plan sourcing, an assigned vendor's phone is a tap-to-dial link too — you're usually on site when you need them.
  • Quick edit / Full edit — Quick edit is the contact details (plus website, service area and notes), saves just what you changed, and is the one to use from a phone. It carries both contacts in full (name, title, mobile, email) as well as the office line, because "the owner gave me his direct email" happens standing in front of the vendor, not at a desk. Full edit → opens the complete record below.
  • Edit (full) — intake/vetting fields: category group, category, the firm's office phone / office email / website / address / service area / licence, two contacts (see §13b), pricing structure, ballpark, minimum job, lead time, COI on file, notes.
  • Performance is rated post-job (a rolling average), kept separate from the curated fields so a vetting edit never wipes performance history.
  • Delete (manager PIN) — inside the Edit form, Delete this vendor permanently removes the row from the app and the spreadsheet. It's PIN-gated (the same manager PIN as approvals/client-delete) and reserved for bulk-import errors or defunct companies — not a substitute for Do Not Use, which is for real vendors you're retiring. A vendor with job history (a rating or completed jobs) is refused and pointed to Do Not Use instead, so a delete can't orphan performance history. That is asked when you press it and again after the PIN (since 2026-09-30, as the contractor delete does); it was asked on the button only, so a vendor rated on a job while the PIN box was open went through.
Adding a vendor: one press, one row. The Save button disables itself and reads Saving… while the write is in flight, and an addVendor is deliberately never queued or auto-retried — a re-send would append the row a second time — so a second press while the first is running is the one way a duplicate reaches the sheet. It happened on 2026-08-27 against a slow web app. Three guards now: the disabled button, a refusal to add a name the directory already holds (one firm is one row; the name is what resolves a vendor to a job, so two rows under one name break every later lookup), and a 45-second watchdog that re-enables the button, reloads the directory and says the row may already have been written rather than leaving the form stuck on Saving…. A failed add says the same thing — a failed POST never reveals whether it reached Google, so “failed” on its own is what invites the duplicating press.

13a. Category Group & Category (how a vendor is filed)

Every vendor is filed under a Category group (required, exactly one) and one or more Categories. The five groups are the fixed top-level taxonomy; categories live underneath them and are open-ended — you add a new one just by typing it.

  • A vendor that does several things is ONE row, with its trades separated by a semicolon — Jewelry & Watch Appraiser; Jewelry & Watch Buyer. One row means one rating, one status, one phone number and one contact history, so a call logged against the firm is visible whichever trade you found it under. It is listed under every one of its categories in the Vendors tree, and counted once in its group header. Semicolon, not comma — eleven category names contain /, eight contain & and one contains a comma; none contains a semicolon.
  • To add a trade to a firm already in the directory, use Edit on its card — do not add a second row. The app refuses a second row under a name already present, because a vendor assigned to a job is resolved by name: two rows under one name make every later lookup pick between them at random.
  • The group dictates the FIRST category. Pick a group first; the Category field then offers only that group's existing categories, which prevents nonsensical pairings (e.g. a mover filed under "Document Shredding"). Past the first semicolon the suggestions widen to every category in the directory, because the reason you are typing a second trade is that it is a different kind of work.
  • Adding a new category is self-serve. If none of the group's categories fit a new specialty vendor (e.g. a stone/hardwood Floor Refinishing outfit), just type the new category name — it's created under the selected group. No developer/code change needed.
  • The group decides which estimate menu — and fee — the vendor feeds (see table). A category added under a group automatically shows up in that group's estimate menu and, once a vendor in it is Active, is offered in the matching Job Plan sourcing picker.
Category groupFeeds estimate menuFee
Property PreparationProperty Preparation card → the Home Prep list (§5e / §6a)30% Home Sale Preparation Fee on every engagement, standalone or bundled onto a labor job (changed 2026-09-10). Vendors bill the client at cost; the 30% is Havellin's, and coordinating them books no concierge hours (declutter hours are separate, §6c). On Home Prep a change order can add one mid-job (since 2026-09-30, §9).
Asset Liquidation & ValuationIts own card → third-party vendor list (§5d)At cost — no fee
Disposal & Waste ManagementIts own card, and several of its categories also appear on the End-of-Job Logistics cardAt cost — no fee
Moving & LogisticsIts own card → third-party vendor listAt cost — no fee
Professional ServicesIts own card → third-party vendor listAt cost — no fee

The sixth estimate card, End-of-Job Logistics, is not a Category Group — it is a curated list of five end-of-job categories, drawn across the groups. A category can therefore appear on two cards; adding the line from either one produces the same vendor line.

What stays fixed: the five groups and the group→menu routing above. Everything below them — the categories — is self-serve, so onboarding a new type of vendor never requires a code change.

A multi-trade vendor still has only ONE group, and on a firm whose trades span two groups that is a real limitation. The group is what routes a vendor to a Build Estimate card and to a fee (the table above), so an estate sale company that also hauls junk gets filed under whichever group you chose and its other trade appears on that same card rather than on Disposal & Waste Management. It is not lost — the vendor is still findable, still assignable, and the Job Plan resolves it by name — but file the firm under the group matching the work you engage it for most, and expect its second trade on that card. Most multi-trade firms are unaffected: an appraiser who also buys is Asset Liquidation & Valuation on both counts.

An appraiser who also buys or sells is flagged, and the Independent box is unticked for you. On the Job Admin & Inv tab's appraiser roster, the directory picker marks such a firm ⚠ also Jewelry & Watch Buyer and, when you pick it, unticks Independent and says why. A firm that may end up acquiring the property — buying it outright, or taking it to auction or an estate sale for a commission on the price — cannot give a defensible opinion of its value. The box stays editable: whether that matters on this estate is your call, not the app's. Before 2026-09-09 the option label hid the buying trade and the box shipped ticked, so the roster asserted Independent over precisely the firm that was not — re-check the roster on any job where an appraiser was linked from the directory before that date.

13b. The office line and the people behind it (added 2026-09-18)

A vendor is a firm, so the record separates what belongs to the company from what belongs to a person. Anthony's case: southflorida@navismoving.com is the company's inbox, and andy@navismoving.com is the owner's — and before this there was one email box, so you kept one and lost the other.

FieldWhose it isWhat it's for
Office phone · Office emailThe firmThe switchboard and the general inbox. Reaches whoever picks up.
Contact 1 / Contact 2 — first, last, titleA personWho to ask for. The title is the useful half: Owner and Dispatch are different calls.
Contact 1 / Contact 2 — mobile, emailThat personTheir own cell and their own address. Never the firm's.
What it fixes, and it was a real defect rather than a missing box. The card used to print the contact's name at the head of the office number — a switchboard presented as that person's direct line, the same mistake as printing the Havellin office line as a concierge's personal mobile (§7). The office row carries no name now and says office beside the number; each person gets their own row saying mobile beside theirs.
Nothing was renamed, so nothing migrated. The existing phone and email columns still hold the office line and the general inbox — only their labels changed — and Contact 1 keeps the contact_first / contact_last columns it always had. No Apps Script redeploy is needed for vendors: the vendor sheet creates any column it is asked to write, so the new fields appear on the existing sheet by themselves.
Fill in whichever half you have. A slot with a number and no name still counts — that is a card you were handed and half-typed, and the app would rather keep the number than insist on a name; the card says name not recorded so you know what's missing. A slot with a title and nothing else does not count, because there is nobody to reach. A vendor with nothing but an office line reads exactly as it always did.

14. Referral Partners

Tab: Referral Partners. A CRM for the network who refer business: estate attorneys, realtors (Douglas Elliman agents among them), trust officers, wealth managers, senior living and country club communities, and allied vendors. Mirrors the Vendor Directory: its own Google Sheet + Apps Script, kept apart from job data.

  • Add / Edit a partner — first & last name, type, firm, title, owner and notes, plus the four ways to reach them (see below). First and last name and the type are required; no phone or email is, because many prospects have none published yet. Owner is one of Anthony Graziano · Ashley Jerome · Anthony Graziano Jr.
  • Search · tap-to-contact · Quick edit — the same field pattern as the Vendor Directory: free-text search across name, firm, title, city and every number and address on the record including the assistant's (so marie gunster finds her: every word must match), tap-to-call/text/email on the card one labelled row per party, and a Quick edit carrying the direct line, the mobile and the office number with Full edit → as the escape hatch.
  • Status runs the outreach lifecycle Identified → Contacted → Intro Meeting → Active Partner (with Dormant and Do Not Use). It's set with the color-coded dropdown on the card (under the partner's type, beside the priority badge) — the same pattern as the Vendor Directory. Partners are retired by status rather than deleted.
  • Log outreach — stores the note typed beside it, stamps last-contacted to today, and auto-advances a still-Identified partner to Contacted (never downgrades one further along). The last-contacted date and note show in a highlighted bar right above the button.
  • Dormancy nudge — an engaged partner with no outreach or referral in 90 days is flagged "Needs Nudge."
  • Leaderboard — who sends the most business. A job counts for a partner when a professional referral source and that partner are recorded on the client, at intake or on Edit Client (since 2026-09-30). The job holds the partner’s uid, so re-sorting the sheet never moves a referral; a row with no uid yet is keyed by its sheet row until backfillIds gives it one (§14a). Ranked by won revenue, then referrals.
  • Delete (manager PIN) — inside the Edit form, Delete this partner permanently removes the row from the app and the spreadsheet, PIN-gated like the vendor delete. Reserved for bulk-import errors / defunct contacts, not a replacement for Do Not Use. A partner with referrals attributed to them is refused (steered to Do Not Use) so the leaderboard history stays intact. That is asked when you press it and again after the PIN (since 2026-09-30, as the vendor and contractor deletes do), and it is refused while the client list has not loaded — The client list has not loaded, so the referrals attributed to … cannot be counted yet. Try again once it has. — because an unread list counts none.

14a. Reaching a partner (added 2026-09-18)

A partner is a person, not a firm — four partners at Comiter are already four rows — so this is deliberately not the vendor's two contact slots. What one partner needs is their own line, their cell, the firm's switchboard, and the assistant who books the meeting.

FieldWhose it isNote
Direct lineThemTheir desk. Unchanged column, relabelled.
MobileThemThe only number offered a Text button.
Office phone · ExtensionThe firmSeveral partners at one firm legitimately share the switchboard; the extension is theirs on it.
Assistant / gatekeeper · phone · emailSomebody elseThe name column already existed, buried in the research block; it now sits beside that person's own number and address.
⚠⚠ On roughly half the directory, the "direct line" is the firm's switchboard — and the card now says so. Measured on the 79 partners before this shipped: 32 carried Phone type: Main, meaning the one phone field held a main number, which the card rendered under that person's name and the Call button dialled; 13 partners shared 5 numbers between them. Those rows read firm main line, not direct, in amber, on the card and their button says ☎ Main line. It flags and never refuses — that number is still the only way through — and it clears the moment you move it into Office phone. To find them all: search the Referral Partners tab for main line.
Phone type stops being decoration. It had no reader anywhere in the app until now; it was the workaround for having one phone field. It is the migration signal instead — it is what tells the card which kind of number the legacy value is. Keep it accurate on any row you clean up.
⚠⚠ This one needs an Apps Script redeploy, unlike the vendor half. The Partners sheet is read by column position, so the five new columns had to be appended after every existing one and the backend redeployed before they can be saved. Paste referral-partners-backend.gs, Deploy → Manage deployments → Edit → New version, then run backfillIds once from the editor. It writes the new header cells and gives every row without one a uid, which jobs key their referral partner on since 2026-09-30 (§14); it only fills blanks, so it is safe to re-run. Until that is done, partner contact fields will not save. The vendor half needs nothing.

The as-found shots moved out of Estate Inventory on 2026-09-20, and both folders are shared together. Anthony: “we are going to provide the inventory back to the personal representative and potentially give them access to the folder… but those won’t be tied to any inventory numbers.” They are a different kind of thing — the inventory photographs are one per line and cited by item number on the schedule, the approval request and the receipts, while the as-found shots are cited by nothing and are read only when somebody asks what a drawer held. Forty wide shots of empty rooms sitting between a representative and the photograph of the sideboard they are being asked to authorise is what the split removes. ⚠ A job folder created before 2026-09-20 has no such subfolder and keeps everything in Estate Inventory — nothing breaks and nothing is moved. To split an old job, create a folder called exactly As-Found Record in its Drive folder by hand; the app re-reads Drive when a name is missing and picks it up on the next shot, with nothing to run.

16. Rates & Fees Reference

Billing basis. The living-client services open on time-and-materials — hours × rate, trued to what's logged — and, since 2026-09-30 (Anthony, Q10), Estate Settlement, Probate and Contested Probate open on a firm fixed price (until then every service opened on time-and-materials). Either way the choice is the concierge’s on any individual job, Probate and Contested Probate included as of 2026-09-20. Home Prep for Sale is neither — it's fee-only on managed vendor spend, plus any concierge declutter hours quoted (§6), and has no fixed-price option.

The probate withholding came off because its reason stopped being true, not as a relaxation. It was withheld on the ground that those expenses are reviewed by the court and a flat fee that cannot be tied back to logged time is what gets questioned. Hours are now logged on every job, fixed price included and mandatory for the concierge (§12) — so the record exists on a flat-fee probate exactly as it does on a time-and-materials one; the fee is simply not billed from it. Anthony, 2026-09-20: “executors or personal representatives are going to want to know a number, not here’s my best estimate of hours and I could go over, and if I go over by 15% I’ll just keep getting change orders to you.” Nothing about the court’s review of the fee is waived by quoting one, and the contingency on those two stays wider than everywhere else (§5c).

On a fixed price a change order is triggered by a change in scope, never by the hours, and it carries its price (2026-09-25, §9). The 15% notice duty belongs to time and materials alone: on a flat fee an overrun on the agreed scope is ours to absorb, which is the point of the fee. The price is the change order’s hours at the job’s own rates (standard or Premium Estate), rounded once, and the final invoice adds exactly that figure.

⚠ Since 2026-09-30 a change order on an expedited fixed price carries the 20% premium too (Anthony, the Q14 follow-up: “yes”): $2,000 of hours prints as $2,400. It applies on an estimate saved from that date, whose premium is its own line, and the rate is pinned on the change order when it is raised — so one raised before then, or on an older fee with the premium inside it, keeps its plain-rate price. The preferred-client discount never applies to a change order. On time and materials a change order carries hours and no price; the hours log bills them. On Home Prep for Sale a change order carries concierge declutter hours at the printed rate, a prep vendor (since 2026-09-30), or both: once the client accepts, the vendor joins the job’s prep vendors, bills the client directly at cost, and the 30% Home Sale Preparation Fee applies to what it actually charges (§9).

What we bill the client

ItemStandardPremium Estate
Transition Concierge$150/hr$185/hr
Property Specialist$100/hr$125/hr
Premium specialty coordination—None since 2026-10-01 (P17): Premium is the rates only. It was a flat +25 concierge hrs; each channel is its own vendor line now (§5d-i)
Billing incrementsEstimates in whole hours, rounded up; hours logged in half hours and billed as logged; change orders in whole hours; money to the cent (since 2026-10-02; quarter hours for P17’s one day)Same
Service Management Fee (third-party vendors)None — at costNone — at cost
Vendor coordination (per vendor line)touches × 0.5 = 0.5–6.0 concierge hrs, billed at the rate above (§5d-i)Same hours, premium rate

Home Prep declutter hours (§6a-i)Concierge rate, $150/hr, trued to the hours logged$185/hr
Moving MaterialsFixed package price — None / $200 / $350 / $500 / $550 / $750 / $1,500, picked on the estimateSame packages
Preferred Client DiscountHavellin labor only (on a fixed price, the fee less the materials package) · max 15%Same · max 15%
Expedited delivery (rush order)+20% of Havellin services, never of the 30% prep fee (on a fixed price, of the fee and, since 2026-09-30, of each change order’s price)Same
Fixed-price contingency+20% (Probate +25%, Contested Probate +35%) on the services priced at two specialists — never lower, whatever crew runs the job (Q5, 2026-09-30) — excluding the home prep fee, which sits on top; no expedite or discount inside it (their own lines since 2026-09-30)Same, at the premium rates

The rush premium is charged on the Havellin services total less the 30% home prep fee (on a fixed price, on the fee, and on each change order raised since 2026-09-30 — above), before any Preferred Client Discount, and never on vendor costs; since 2026-09-30 (Q9), an estimate saved before then is billed as it was quoted. Hourly rates stay as published — the premium is a separate line, so a client comparing us to another firm sees standard NASMM-range rates plus a clearly-labelled expedite.

Order of operations on the price, because a discount and an expedite on the same job is a fair question to ask:

Hourly: Havellin services total → plus 20% expedited delivery (not on the prep fee) → less Preferred Client Discount (labor fees and the premium charged on them) → plus vendor costs at cost = total project estimate
Fixed price: the fixed fee → plus 20% of the fee → less Preferred Client Discount (the fee less the materials package, and the premium charged on it) → plus the 30% home prep fee → plus vendor costs at cost = total project estimate

The premium comes first and the discount after, since 2026-09-11: a discount is a decision about the fee, not about the calendar, so it must never shrink the expedited-delivery charge, and on a rush job it comes off the labour grossed up by the premium charged on it (Anthony: “If it’s a hundred thousand dollar job on labor, and we apply a twenty percent premium … that’s $120k. A 10% discount is $12k.”). Corrected here 2026-09-23; this paragraph and the flow above still described the old order. On a fixed-price quote the contingency is the only thing inside the fee (2026-09-30, Anthony on Q9 and Q13): the fee is the services, priced at two specialists, plus the contingency; the 20% premium is then its own line on the fee, the discount its own line off the fee less the materials package (and the premium charged on it), and the 30% home prep fee sits on top with no premium on it (§5c). Until then the contingency was applied last, to a figure that already contained both, and a fixed fee saved before 2026-09-30 carries its premium and discount inside it and is billed as it was quoted. An hourly quote has no such floor, deliberately: it bills the hours worked, and a bigger crew bills fewer concierge hours, so it dips at each automatic crew step (Anthony, 2026-09-30: “we don’t want jobs stretching over weeks, so we bump up crew size to keep work days down”).

The margin panel is hidden on Build Estimate as of 2026-08-02 — it was too much detail for the tab the estimate is built on. Nothing was deleted and nothing changed about how margin is calculated; the panel and its underlying figures are still computed on every recalculation. Two consequences to know about: the Price at 30% Margin / Above Reference pair is no longer in front of you when you set a discount, and neither is the warning that fires when the 50% deposit stops covering job cost. The 15% discount cap is the guardrail that remains, and it is enforced on both routes to a discount.

When it is read, read it for what it is: an indicative profitability readout with a flag on it, not a discounting tool. Price at 30% Margin is a reference line at an admittedly arbitrary margin, and Above Reference is simply the distance to it. Neither is an allowance to spend. In practice a job that needs that much discount is one to walk away from rather than one to price down to.

What we pay — labour cost rates (internal, never billed)

These are a different set of numbers from the table above, and confusing the two is expensive in both directions — billing a client at cost, or paying a contractor at the billing rate. Cost rates feed the margin panel only (hidden on Build Estimate since 2026-08-02, still computed). Settings holds the defaults for new estimates (Assumed Crew Cost, Section 2); a saved estimate keeps the rates it was priced with (since 2026-09-02; one saved before then reads the current Settings). The values below are the defaults.
RoleCostApplies to
Founder concierge$100/hrAnthony, Ashley
Contractor concierge$60/hrAn outside concierge — the Contractor — TC placeholder slot
Contractor PS — Standard$30/hrThe Contractor — Standard placeholder slot
Contractor PS — Senior$35/hrThe Contractor — Senior placeholder slot

Cost follows the person, not the job title. Founders and contractors are paid differently for the same role, so a named individual in the Contractors directory is always costed at their own rate (§12a). The four rates above cover only the unnamed placeholder slots you staff before a specific person is assigned.

If a concierge can't be resolved to a person at all, the margin panel costs them at the founder rate — deliberately the most expensive assumption, so margin is never overstated — and says on the panel that the figure is an assumption. Assign the concierge, or set their rate on the Contractors tab, to replace it with a real number.

16a. Win / Loss

A row on the Client Dashboard, under Total Jobs — it was its own tab until 2026-09-23 (§9, The client list). Reads the client's decision, not ours. A job counts as won when it has been marked Won on the dashboard — or, for jobs that predate that step, when it reached Active or Closed under the old model, so the existing history isn't rewritten.

What that changes in practice: a job sitting at Approved — Awaiting Client is not in the won column. It used to be, which meant the win rate counted internal approvals. Expect the number to read lower and truer than it did.

Loss reasons are captured at closeout — price, scope, timing, unresponsive, competitor, other — with an optional note (competitor name, price gap). Closed — Deposit Retained jobs stay on the won side, because they produced revenue; keeping them separate from ordinary losses is why the two statuses exist. They count at what they kept, not their quote (2026-09-29) — what was received less any refund recorded (2026-10-01) — and any money received on any stage makes a walkaway retained rather than lost (§9).

17. Manager PINs

Agreement approval came off this list on 2026-09-10. There is no second PIN on the agreement any more — meeting its conditions (estimate approved, client marked Won, and since 2026-09-29 no raised price still waiting for the client’s re-acceptance) stamps the approval as the packet is printed, sent or filed. See §8 for why that is safe, and do not restore the gate as a precaution.

Required for: estimate approval · estimate denial · a final invoice more than ±15% off the estimate (§12 — deposit and midpoint invoices need none; the approval is kept with that client, for that balance, and asks again if the balance moves) · deleting a client, a vendor, a referral partner or (since 2026-09-30) a contractor.

A PIN never deletes a directory record with history. A vendor with a rating or completed jobs, a referral partner with referrals attributed, and a contractor named on any job are refused and retired instead — Do Not Use for a vendor or a partner, Inactive for a contractor (§12a–§14). The question is asked on the ✕ and again behind the PIN (since 2026-09-30; a vendor or a partner used to be asked on the ✕ only), so a rating or a referral that lands while the PIN box is open still stops the delete; and the partner and contractor deletes are refused while the client list has not loaded, since what they count is on the jobs.

Not required for a Change Order (§9) — that one is settled with the client, not internally.

Each person has their own PIN, and the approval is recorded under whoever typed it. Previously there was one shared code and the approver was stored as a fixed name, so an estimate Ashley approved was filed under Anthony's — worse than recording nothing, because it read as evidence. Contact management for current PINs.

A PIN here is attribution, not security. The app is a single file served from the web, so anyone who views the page source can read the PINs. It raises the bar against the wrong person clicking approve; it does not stop anyone determined. Real authentication needs a server and is a separate project. Don't treat a PIN as protecting anything valuable on its own.
